ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e218	Initially, I would also like to commend the rapporteur-in-Office, Hans Olav Syversen, for an orderly way of exercising the Rules of Procedure. I would say that we spend too little of our valuable speaking time giving praise when it's earned and someone should have praise. In very many areas it has been possible to find common notes, where both the government parties and all or part of the opposition are involved. Then it's true that there are areas where opposition parties with varying degrees of support have different proposals. It is also worth noting that in many important areas the Progress Party is alone on both comments and a number of proposals. So move on to the real content of the government message. I want to start by focusing on the ethical guidelines of the State pension fund – abroad. The evaluation process has been under way in 2008. What is now being presented on the basis of concrete proposals has been achieved on the basis of extensive work and not least a comprehensive background. The exposure has been large. For example, more than 50 national and foreign hearing bodies have made extensive recordings. I have the impression that unfortunately this work has drowned a little in other issues relating to the State pension fund, not least because 2008 has been a challenging year as a result of the financial crisis. The Ethical Guidelines were unanimously endorsed in the United Kingdom in 2004 and have since gained considerable recognition in the country and abroad. Many have shown what Norway is doing in this area, but we are not going to be too self-sufficient. I think, through good ethical practice, we can make others follow. Through the communication on the State pension fund, which we are now dealing with, the Government is putting up a broader and even more active focus on ethical management. It is suggested that the environment, social conditions and good corporate governance should be more integrated as important factors throughout the management of the Fund. So far, the two most important measures have been an active ownership exercise in the companies we are in, and exclusion of companies from the investment universe of the fund. And of course, we're going to keep doing that. I am happy and proud of the concrete measures that the red-green government is now putting up with. This applies not least to the exclusion of tobacco-producing companies from the portfolio, the establishment of an observation list as a new measure against companies in the border zone for the extraction and strengthening of concrete ownership, by formulating several expectations documents, both in the environmental field and in the corporate governance area. Procedures shall also be established for the re-entry into the portfolio of companies that have been excluded. Climate has been an extremely important area of work, both for the Government and for the hearing bodies. Both expectations documents on the climate strategy of companies and a broad investigation addressing the climate challenges in financial markets should be initiated and how investors should act on this basis. An environmental programme shall be established to target investments aimed at achieving a clear environmental benefit, such as climate-friendly energy, energy efficiency improvement, carbon capture/storage, water technology, etc. Then I record that the Progress Party will not have more stringent ethical guidelines, and that the Progress Party and the Right will not agree with the majority's remark to exclude shares from tobacco producers from the fund's investment universe. So over to two other areas, which I expect will be at the heart of today's debate. It is the development of the fund's portfolio as a result of the financial crisis and issues of active and passive management. There is no reason to suggest that 2008 was a bad year for the State pension fund and the worst year since the fund was established. Developments have clearly become ‘some smears’ as a result of the international financial crisis. It also says something when one in the international stock markets has not experienced a weaker return for a single year than one experienced last year. If we look at the fund's return measured in international currency, the foreign part's return was over 23 pst. i minus last year. It is also part of the picture that the value of the fund increased by NOK 256 billion in 2008. The increase is linked to a weaker price and, in particular, to the introduction of petroleum revenues, while the return, as is known, fell sharply. Then I would like to stress that the state is a long-term investor, and experience has paid long-term and patient investors well to take risks in times of major market turmoil. One important element is that we invest with our own money. We don't invest with borrowed money. Many investors are forced to sell when prices are low. It's not the Norwegian state. It's said that short-term owners have short-term thoughts, and that it's the opposite of the long-term owners. Yes, it is that the country has long-term thoughts about the State pension fund – abroad. In 2008, the SPU had a strong increase in ownership in international markets, at almost 0.8 p.p., compared with 2007. This growing ownership of enterprises worldwide could generate future profits from listed companies. The active administration has over time focused on and criticised both politically and mediatically. I think it's important that we bring with us the history when we go into that debate. Until 2007, there were nine years of positive returns compared to the return on the reference portfolio. Then there has been a smaller return in the active government, especially in 2008, where more people have been involved. Many critics, including in political circles, have argued that the negative return in 2008 is lost money, and that politicians have wasted much of Norway’s oil wealth. It is wrong to say that the money is lost. Most of it is unrealized losses. Only several years in advance will we be able to say whether the active administration has given a positive return. However, we should be humble in this situation, and both the Bank of Norway itself and the Ministry have taken several measures to improve risk management. The international financial crisis has revealed many areas in which legislation needs to be strengthened or made more robust. Things can change quickly. Not least, we look at it from the State pension fund – abroad. The fund had, as mentioned earlier, a minus return of more than 23 pst. in 2008, and the active administration generated a minus return of 3,4 p.p., measured in international currency. Also in the first quarter of this year, the fund had some negative figures, but when we look at the second quarter, the development has been significantly better, and one should not be any great genius or rocket scientist to see that a positive contribution from the active government can be expected in the second quarter. I don't want to dimension this more up than to say that this is a positive and joyful trend. The sharp criticism from some teams that we have lost over 600 billion kroner on the active administration seems – as far as I can see – to have stopped anything. At least it will be exciting to see if the trend continues beyond the year. The risk framework for active management of the State pension fund — abroad has been strongly focused in the public debate. Here, as is known, there is a quantitative framework for the active management of Norway’s Bank, with an upper limit of so-called expected relative volatility of 1.5 percentage points. This limit says something about how far from the expected average return on the market we will accept that the fund is. As the Governor briefed us at the hearing, and as we were informed by the Ministry, the risk measurement system showed that in some cases the risk associated with the active management in the fourth quarter of 2008 exceeded the relative volatility limit, which is set at 1.5 pst. In view of this, the Ministry of Finance has stepped up its reporting obligation and has asked for ongoing reporting of developments in this field. The explanation from Norway’s Bank is that the event must be seen in the context of events in the financial markets and large locked-in positions in the interest rate portfolio. It is also underlined that the exceedance was not triggered by certain individual actions by the bank. It is also stated that the expected relative volatility has now fallen below the limit again, and according to the quarterly report of Norway’s Bank, which came on 20 May of this year, we can read that this was 0.78 percentage points at the end of the first quarter. With regard to the situation, I feel that both the Norwegian Bank itself and not least the Department and the Council of State are taking important and necessary action with regard to weaknesses in the system. The Bank of Norway acknowledges, as mentioned above, that the financial crisis posed major challenges in terms of risk management and risk management, and sees weaknesses in the implementation of the investment strategy. A number of measures have been launched by the Bank of Norway to address the weaknesses, for example through a reinforced follow-up to capital management and also to the bond portfolio, where the problems have been greatest and the results are weakest. This has not been made as clear in the public debate. The Ministry takes important action both in the short and longer term in relation to active management. This is done through both more new and, not least, more stringent requirements. In the near future, the Ministry will issue a draft comprehensive legislation at the hearing. New legislation on the management of the Fund is provided for as from 1 January 2010. It is also announced that an external review will be carried out in which specific issues relating to active management will be subject to a comprehensive assessment. The results of this review will be presented in the spring of 2010. So it's taking hold in the short term, and it's taking hold of a slightly longer time horizon. In my opinion, it is necessary to put in place measures that can take place more quickly than until the time when we have an external review completed. It makes sense, in my view, to have a two-stage process in relation to this issue. We know that by sending a case out to the hearing, it can take many months for a person to get it back and the department can start working on the matter. With this, I recommend Stortinget to join the Committee's position.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e319	The chord shift has been completed.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e322	First, I would also like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done. I would like to stress that the Progress Party and the Christian People's Party actually agree on a number of points regarding the management message of the year – for the very first time, as far as I know. The fourth year’s management of the Staten Pensjonfond – abroad was disastrously poor. I'm a little surprised that there's only one journalist present in this room today, because these are sensational numbers. 633 billion kroner were lost. I call it lost. You can lose as much as you can lose less. Then I'd rather say like Øystein Stray Spätalen: I calculate what I have, and what I can sell it for. Today has a NOK 633 billion less than one had last year, per. 1 January. It's going to be 25 pst. of the management capital — it is gone. Although this is not entirely comparable, to put things in perspective, I would like to remind you that the total additional consumption of the Progress Party over its alternative state budgets on tax relief and investment in infrastructure, transport, defence equipment, police equipment and research equipment for universities, over the 12-year lifetime of the Oil Fund, has been around. DKK 210 billion. So for this sum, we would have a more modern infrastructure, a more competitive business and about. 150 billion more in private welfare. But the Progress Party also understands that 2008 was an exceptionally bad year for investment in financial markets, and we understand that the fund lost money. We can therefore also understand the frustration of the Governor when he said that there was no hiding anywhere. What we do not accept, however, is that the Minister of Finance is trying to minimize the financial loss that the Government has inflicted on the Norwegian people. It's itself a scandal. After she had presented such disastrous figures, the Progress Party had expected a far more humble finance minister than we have experienced. We had actually assumed that the Government would now listen to the Advance Party's repeated warnings against the fund's risk profile, but unfortunately we experience the opposite. Instead, the Minister of Finance takes on the role of the speculator and explains and defends each and every lost crown with the fund's long-term investment horizon. The slogan ‘when in trouble — double’ should not govern any administrators, and that is in any case not how we should manage our common wealth. The Bank of Norway has the possibility, by way of regulation, to operate so-called active management. It implies that the fund's managers are allowed to attempt to make investments that yield better returns than the average in the market. The assumption is that Norway’s Bank does not exceed a total risk limit — expected relative volatility — of 1.5 pst. The deviation framework applies to the overall framework of the Fund, allowing the Bank of Norway to choose active management of all or part of the Fund, as long as the overall risk framework of the Fund does not exceed 1,5 pst. However, the Norwegian Bank breached this risk limit in October 2008. This is mainly due to the fact that the SPU made very unfavourable investments in interest-bearing papers, and that the SPU was holding bonds that could not be traded in the market over time. The negative return on active management for 2008 was DKK 88 billion. That's $18,000 per. North. So this is not a loss due to the general decline in the market, but the money we have lost because the investments that were made were worse than the average in the market. This is the money we would not have lost if we had put the money in a banknote box. This is an incredible amount of money. Where have the media really been in this process? This implies that the average annual lower rate of return from the active administration has been 0,04 pst. However, this figure does not include the costs associated with such management, so the actual annual lower rate of return has been 0.13 pst. In figures, the loss associated with the active management over the lifetime of the fund amounts to DKK 64,5 billion over 12 years. This amount is equal to the values of the State's shares in Telenor, DnB NOR, Kongsberg Gruppe and Aker Solutions. Following the presentation of the annual results for the SPU, a number of professionals have expressed themselves with scepticism to SPU’s active management. These have claimed that active management over time will not yield better returns than index-based management. The available evidence confirms these claims. Some have argued that SPU has its own responsibility for active management because of its size. This is the Progress Party disagrees with, and underlines that there are funds with higher administrative capital than the SPU that have chosen index-based management. However, the Progress Party recognises that there can be arguments in favour of active management continuing, and we therefore support the Government's informed review of the benefits and disadvantages of such management. However, there is no reason for the Government to use a full year to elaborate on these arguments. An overall opposition therefore proposes that the Government speed up the review and present it as its own case for the autumn. An overall opposition also asks the Government to set a framework for the active administration which limits such management to what is strictly necessary for commercial reasons until this assessment has been carried out. Active management incurs much higher administrative costs than index-based management. The Progress Party is therefore surprised that Norway’s Bank chooses to report its results before costs. Such a practice would be totally unimaginable in any other industry. The Progress Party therefore promotes, together with the Right, a proposal that results in the SPU will be reported in the future according to costs under Global Investment Performance Standards – GIPS. The proposals promoted by the Progress Party through the management communication of the year have three main objectives: to reduce the risk the fund is exposed to in order to better facilitate Norwegian industry and business to make each individual manager more responsible for the risk to which he or she exposes the people's savings to These changes will more closely address the Fund’s objective of achieving the highest possible management at moderate risk. Over time, there has been a wide divergence between the Government's words and the Government's actions regarding the risk exposure in the management of the SPU. This is due, inter alia, to: the following conditions: Investments in emerging markets started in 2000. Investments in interest-bearing papers without a State guarantee started in 2002. In 2007, the equity exposure increased from 40 pst. to 60 pst. Investments in small limited liability companies started in 2007. Investments in unlisted shares started in 2007. Over the period 2001-2008, the ethical guidelines have become increasingly more comprehensive. All of this increases the risk. The government’s challenge and dilemma is that it tries to appear as a serious investor with a high return and moderate risk focus, while on autopilot it follows the investment councils that come from those environments that have a self-interest in expanding the investment universe. The Government insists that such changes in the management strategy can be characterised as risk spreading, but they are in reality increased risk exposure. This is due, inter alia, to: the location, size, volume of turnover and volatility of the investment objects. Yes, you obviously spread the risk if you choose to play horse, football and lotto at the same time. However, whether such a strategy reduces risk exposure is more doubtful. The Progress Party is saddened that in 2007 the Government got rid of all those who argued to increase its share from 40 p.m. to 60 pst. We believe it was an unwise and risky decision. Not only was the time completely wrong, but the modified strategy contributed to an unnecessary increase in the Fund's constant risk exposure. We are therefore re-promoting our 2007 proposal to change the SPU investment strategy. However, we assume that Norway’s Bank takes account of market developments before this is implemented – that is part of the proposal. The Party of Progress notes that the Government chooses to interpret the ethical guidelines more and more closely. So once again, we want to stress that ethics per. definition is a subjective assessment basis that allows for selective and inconsistent exclusion mechanisms. The party of progress does not share the Government’s vision of the practice of the ethical guidelines. Rigid application of the ethical guidelines reduces the fund's investment universes, contributes to increasing the fund's risk and reduces the fund's potential return. Not least, this Government's exclusion from the world's leading defence technology companies. The Progress Party also notes that the Government is increasingly complying with the demands and wishes of small stakeholder organisations aimed at limiting the investment potential of the Pension Fund, thus reducing the fund’s return potential. Despite the government's assurances of the opposite, we find that the government's excessive mix of ethics and shop is increasingly perceived as foreign policy signals. The Progress Party therefore supports the following proposals: ‘The Government asks the Government to review the enterprises currently excluded from the State pension fund – Foreign investment portfolio based on rigorous practices of the ethical guidelines.’ We are facing a situation where the Government first incorporates all the administrative councils that come from leading management environments, and then listen to advice from clever lobbyists who want to exclude more and more products, industries and businesses from SPU's investment universes. Overall, this fund increases its risk profile. This year, the Government chooses to exclude tobacco companies from the portfolio of investments. This is justified by the fact that tobacco used as intended leads to sickness and death. No good news from there, in other words. However, in his narrow quest to appear ethically correct, the Government forgets two important issues. Firstly, tobacco provides hundreds of millions of people with pleasure and well-being in everyday life — it is also an ethical choice — and secondly, tobacco cultivation gives millions of poor farmers a living. But such matters are obviously not included in the Government's ethical accounts. They kind of don't fit in. If this had not been the last administrative message this Government is sending out, we'd be able to expect the exclusion of industries that produce sweets – for what do you really want with that, you get holes in your teeth? And gambling services are creating sad family feuds. Alcohol causes liver damage, and there's no way that people can find out when they're drunk. Publishers that produce unnecessary literature – what about it, it just steals the time for people? Or what about companies that produce products that contribute to increased CO2 emissions – then we can let go of investments in car manufacturers and aircraft manufacturers? And what about businesses without 40 pst. representation of women in the board of directors, or enterprises that do not have any management minorities? Or what about climate changes like the oil and gas industry? No, by the way, it hadn't happened, because then we had no money to invest at all! The Progress Party promoted in its 2008 and 2009 Alternative State Budget proposals that Norway establish a fund in developing countries with the aim of contributing to the creation of sustainable business. We record that this idea has gradually been supported by other parties. A little later today, the Stortinget will debate St.meld. No 13, and then the Progress Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left will propose the creation of a fund of approximately DKK 10 billion for this purpose. At the same time, the Progress Party notes that the Government intends to establish an investment programme of DKK 20 billion linked to environmental investment opportunities – a programme proposed by the Government to be linked to ‘sustainable investments’ and to include unlisted companies and technologies in emerging markets. The Progress Party therefore chooses to interpret it in such a way that there is broad political consensus on such a fund initiative or programme. However, the Progress Party considers that there is a need for the Government to clarify the premises for any fund or investment programme. We therefore want to clarify whether the Government wants both an investment programme and a fund for sustainable business, and in that case what mandates and focus such a programme or fund should have. In this context, it should be clarified whether such a fund/investment programme should aim at achieving financial returns, reducing environmental and climate emissions, helping more generally, helping to strengthen local business, or being a doorway for Norwegian energy companies and Norwegian technology. Furthermore, it is appropriate to clarify whether such a bet should be made as part of the State pension fund – Foreign with the mandates granted to SPU, or whether such a fund should be separated by the SPU and, where appropriate, given other mandates. As the proposal for an investment programme now exists, the Progress Party will choose to go against the programme, not least because we are sceptical of the Minister of Finance's assurances that the programme will yield at least as good returns as other parts of the fund, as she stated in a debate with me in the News of the Day, Athens, because it would be thought that investors were in a position to invest in these programmes. The refusal of the Council of State to establish its own fund for the purpose also underpins our scepticism. The Party considers that a possible investment programme in developing countries should address a number of issues. Not least, such a programme should be able to promote the interests of Norway as an energy nation. The Progress Party is therefore promoting an alternative proposal. Financial management has always been a very lucrative industry to work in. For this reason, we find many of the country's best paid within just capital management. State pension fund – Foreigners have also established a similar remuneration system. The average basic salary is approx. One million. kr, while the bonus payments may be the multiple. Norway's Bank stated that even in a year when the fund lost NOK 633 billion, it was paid approx. 20 bonuses of over 2 million. kr. Norway’s Bank is by no means a payroll in the industry, but at the same time, SPU differs from other capital management precisely because the capital it manages is transferred automatically on the basis of Norway’s profits on oil and gas sales. In other words, an administrator in Norway’s Bank does not need to spend time or effort on the least attractive part of the job, namely to raise investment capital in the market. Thus, a job in Norway’s Bank Investment Management, NBIM, becomes in many ways the dream job of every medium-sized manager, with guaranteed minimum income and large bonus payments if investments do better than the reference index. The Progress Party considers such gold-plated wage and bonus payments to be unreasonable and believes that each administrator must be more financially liable for the risk he or she takes with the Norwegian people's savings. The Progress Party considers that there should be as far as possible a convergence of interests between the owners of the capital and the managers. Ideally, therefore, a bonus program should have both an upside and a downside. The Progress Party therefore supports the following proposals: ‘The Government asks the Government to instruct the Bank of Norway to enter into working arrangements with its administrators for the future, which also include downside bonuses. This shall also apply to external AIFMs with which NBIM agrees.’;» The Progress Party is concerned that Norway’s Bank’s management of the State pension fund — Foreign — has gradually become so extensive that it can go at the expense of the traditional tasks of Norway’s Bank. As an increasing proportion of the Bank's employees have tasks related to the management of the pension fund, there is a risk that the Bank will focus less on financial stability and analysis. The current arrangements may also result in an unfortunate link between the Ministry of Finance and the management of the Fund. The Progress Party agrees that there were good reasons why Norway’s Bank was given the task of managing the oil fund when it was established in 1996, since the central bank had some experience of debt securities and managed to keep an arm’s length distance from political detail management. Today, more and more questions arise as to whether Norway’s Bank has had sufficient capacity to follow up its investments, and many believe that the fund has grown too large for the bank. The management company of Norway’s Bank, NBIM, currently employs 217 permanent staff, of which an estimated 55 are employed by NBIM’s foreign offices in New York, London and Shanghai. 96 persons are linked to the investment activity, while the other 121 employees are linked to operational departments. Furthermore, the Bank of Norway estimates that a total of 400 external annual works are related to the management activities, and these relate exclusively to the active management. So that's where the bonuses are paid. This constitutes a significant activity, far from the core business of a central bank. This suggests that these activities should be assessed by the Bank of Norway. At the same time, it is not possible to change this structure overnight, and alternative management models and structures need to be explored and then put in place before changes can be made, if this is considered necessary. The Progress Party therefore wants an evaluation of the current management of the State pension fund — Foreign countries to identify better ways of doing this work, and we are promoting proposals to this effect. The Progress Party has argued over several years that the management of the SPU should be more effective in Norway. Just as most people take it as a matter of course that Norway manages its own natural resources, so we should also be able to manage the capital that this resource provides. Capital is now one of Norway's comparative advantage, and we should therefore be able to use our new resource to build a leading capital manager environment in Norway. The Progress Party therefore also supports proposals to this effect. The Progress Party also believes that the time is now ripe to assess whether SPU should be split into several smaller funds. Such smaller funds could have different mandates and different risk profiles. Such smaller funds could also lead to competition between the different parts of the fund. Not least, such fragmentation could reduce the risk in the fund. The Progress Party therefore proposes that the Government carry out such an investigation. As a natural extension of this mission, we also suggest that the Government investigate alternative investments for the SPU. For example, such a process could include investments in other commodities, such as gold. The main objective will be to reduce the risk to which the fund is exposed. The size is to a small extent given an independent report on whether the management of the SPU is in line with decisions and guidelines adopted by the Stortinget. Annual management messages cannot be said to be independent and therefore do not contribute to the proper performance of the control function of the Storting. The debate on last year’s management shows that a number of issues related to losses, bonuses, administrative costs and increased secrecy have been raised. In particular, there has been uncertainty as to how it has been possible for the Fund to go beyond the risk limit set by the Ministry of Finance. The Progress Party therefore supports, together with the Right and Left, the proposal that the Riksrevision carry out a study on whether the management of the SPU in 2008 has been in line with the guidelines and decisions adopted by the Stortinget. The Progress Party, together with the Left, also supports proposals to amend the Central Bank Act in order to allow the National Audit Office to carry out in practice an annual audit of the activities of the Bank of Norway in general and the management of the SPU by the Bank of Norway in particular and to report to the Stortinget in the light of this control. The Progress Party further refers to Section 9 of the National Audit Act that the Riksrevision is responsible for auditing the government accounts and all state funds. As most of the state’s capital is managed by the Bank of Norway, the Progress Party believes that an overall audit mechanism under the National Audit Office will contribute overall to the best understanding, understanding and reporting to the grantor. Furthermore, this would be best in accordance with the applicable National Audit Act and the Constitution. The Progress Party therefore promotes in the context of Ot.prp. No 58, to be discussed — probably not much debated — in Odelstinget later this week, proposals for the Riksrevision to take over the audit of the Bank of Norway. The Bank of Norway shall also have an internal audit report to the Governing Council. The internal audit shall assess the Bank's internal control, procedures and other matters relevant to the Bank's operations. President – I see it has become a new president since I started – I take this up with me the proposals that the Progress Party is promoting in the Innst. S. No 277, either alone or with other parties.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e534	In the last six months, the debate on the Pension Fund has been primarily about loss and impairment, mainly as a consequence of the financial crisis. But there has also been a debate on the bonuses of administrators and on the ethical guidelines for investment. Transparency and debate are in themselves important. When, as politicians, we have agreed to move parts of the oil wealth from the bottom of the sea to the Pension Fund, we depend on people to trust that the money is well managed. Openness and debate are therefore an important key. Broad political consensus is also central. Mainly, there is broad political support – with a significant exception in the Progress Party – for the proposal put forward by the Government, which has been further developed by the case rapporteur in good cooperation with the other parties. There are of course some nuances in this, but I think it is important to underline the broad consensus – which is not a sleeping consensus. I see that there has been a lot of good debate and good judgment in relation to the further development that is now taking place in the management of the Pension Fund. I would therefore like to take this opportunity to thank the rapporteur for a good job. It's not special that a country saves on retirement. What distinguishes Norway from almost all other countries is that in Norway, pension saving occurs primarily through government saving, while in all other countries it is largely through private pension funds. If saving through the State pension fund is to have legitimacy, we depend on the people’s confidence in how the money is managed. Without such trust, the pressure to use the money will only increase. The pressure is of course there today, but of a completely different weight if the confidence in saving is weakened. A policy where money goes much more to current consumption rather than to savings could both control the economy and lead to uncertainty about tomorrow's pensions, and shift heavy burdens on future taxpayers. There are many examples of how countries that, like Norway, have come to great wealth quickly, have put it all over the world. I believe that we basically have reason to be proud of how Norway, and also we politicians, have managed to cut off large parts of the oil revenues in recent years. But as I said, even with the size of the current pension fund, we have only financed about half of the savings pension rights. It is therefore important to continue saving. We, who are supporters of the Pension Fund and the rule of action for the use of pension funds, have a special responsibility to follow the administration with a critical look. High risk, large losses, bonuses and high costs associated with the management of wealth will weaken confidence. The same applies to unethical investments. Of course, we are all concerned with high returns on pension funds, but at the same time we want a good conscience about how the money is invested, by not investing in companies that commit, among other things. grossly unethical behaviour. The right is therefore in favour of the proposal put forward by the Left to ask the National Audit Office to go through the administration of the State pension fund – abroad to check whether it is in line with the guidelines given by the Stortinget. The Storing Message looks ahead, and draws up some new guidelines. That is good, but in my opinion it does not provide a good enough basis for giving a stamp of approval to the administration. A review of the National Audit Office will ensure a more independent review. That proposal has the support of the Progress Party, the Right and the Left, and I am pleased that the Christian People's Party has joined that proposal in today's debate. I would like to add that, although that proposal – I wish I could say against assumption – is being voted down today, it does not mean that the Riksrevision on its own keel can not address this and go through whether the administration has been in line with the guidelines that the government has drawn up. The way in which we have organised the State pension fund – Abroad, has had broad support in Stortinget. The right considers that the organisation has been essentially good, but it is nevertheless important that we assess from time to time whether the organisation will continue to be the best in the future. The fund has now become one of the world's largest funds. There are, of course, many arguments for continuing with one large fund, but I note that there is also a technical debate about whether it is optimal to put all eggs in one basket, or whether we should have established several funds in order to have more competing environments. I do not want to draw any conclusions from this today, but the Right makes a proposal, together with the Left, to ask the Government to investigate and evaluate other ways of organising the fund, including whether to maintain one fund or to set up several funds. In another case that is not dealt with today, the Right takes up the proposal to establish a separate board of State pension fund – Foreign. Today, the main board of the Norwegian Bank is the board of directors of the fund. We believe that the competence required to control and monitor the State pension fund – abroad, suggests that there should be a separate board of directors. But we will come back to that when the matter is dealt with in Odelstinget. In our opinion, the result of the active administration is so weak that it weakens confidence in the management of pension funds. Since its inception in 1998, no positive contribution has been made to the return on the Fund. I am pleased that the Government has now announced that it will come back with its own assessment of the active administration in 2010. We believe that it should be speeded up, but we also stress that until this investigation becomes available, active management should be limited to what is strictly necessary, for commercial reasons. We also understand that for business reasons it is not possible to stop all administration until that investigation is available, but we believe that for reasons of trust in administration it should be limited to what is strictly necessary. The establishment of the ethical guidelines is a recognition of the dilemmas we face when we are going to invest so much of the pension money. Of course, we are concerned that there should be a good return on money, and at the same time we are concerned that the money should not be invested in companies that commit unethical acts or act in an unethical way and violate fundamental human rights. At the same time, however, the Pension Fund should not be a foreign policy instrument. It is also important to include in our search for the Pension Fund to act as correctly as possible and as politically as possible. The establishment of the ethical guidelines has been supported by the Right, and further development is mainly supported by the Right. But at the same time, we have to recognise that the guidelines also create some grey areas, which, in turn, will be able to present new challenges. We are therefore concerned that what is of guidelines should be verifiable and that it should not be associated with too much discretion. The right also supports the establishment of a programme within the State pension fund – abroad, which will invest up to DKK 20 billion in the environment and development. However, we stress in our remarks that we do not want to set up too many so-called special programmes within the State pension fund – abroad. We believe it can make it more difficult, or the city of challenges for administrators to measure and follow up different mandates within the same portfolio. It is a problem that we ask the Ministry to consider how to avoid these challenges when establishing a separate programme for the environment and development of the State pension fund – abroad. However, this should be affordable. We believe that it makes sense to establish it, and that will be supported by the Right.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e670	St.meld. No 20 for 2008-2009 on the management of the State pension fund – Foreign is a rarely important notification. We need to learn from our mistakes, and we are in a new era of international economic policy, where this is an important debate, not least in the UN bodies, and it is said that Norway is contributing with its mixed economic tradition here. In 2008, the Fund had significant real losses in securities, including: bankruptcys in companies where the fund was the owner. The loss of the fund is even greater if we look at the book values of the financial securities at the end of the year. In retrospect, it has been found that responsible managers have gone much further in placing our oil money in highly speculative papers, beyond the financial committee. This shall not happen, and responsible persons have taken some maritime criticism. This maritime critique could have been clearer to the Norwegian people. This risky administration was and is part of the spirit of time, free flow of capital — a spirit of time that allowed for large short-term gains, but also very high risks. The supporters of such an international free market economy are represented in Norway with the Progress Party and the Right, and they are also driving international forces behind it. That's why it's kind of strange to hear the posts so far. The centre party is confident that it will invest part of the profits of our oil revenues in the Staten pension fund – abroad. However, when the fund was set up, the Storting raised personal savings to a national virtue and forgot too much – and I stress too much – the real economy. The central party believes that the nation of Norway should save more in the real economy by investing in infrastructure and knowledge, and it is moving in the right direction during this government period. The forthcoming national transport plan is an important contribution. Government pension fund – Foreign manages very large amounts and must have active management. However, it is also important to discuss the framework and implementation of the active management. The allocation of shares and bonds in the fund is claimed to reflect the time horizon of the fund. The Fund’s very long-term time horizon was an important professional argument for raising its share in 2007. The centre party has opted for maritime criticism of this decision in retrospect and acknowledges that the management statement on a so-called long-term perspective resulting from future pension obligations does not imply that the fund has a high share. The centre party prioritises long-term and safe management with less risk exposure and therefore considers it right to reduce its share. The Ministry of Finance and the Bank of Norway assume that in financial markets in which the fund invests, assets are known to be so efficient and are therefore at all times at an approximate price. It won't be like this until the bubbles end, i.e. the gap between financial values and real economic values is closed. It is therefore wrong to argue that the fund today buys so-called cheap shares. In 2008, the active interest rate management of the Bank of Norway caused significant losses due to interest payments made by transaction fees and/or bonus-driven administrators. The parties therefore support the changes notified by Norway’s Bank in the presentation of the 2008 result, as well as its warnings that mandates to external interest rate managers will be withdrawn. Nor is there any reason why our country's fund should be borrowed by investing in loan-funded financial products. Norway’s Bank Investment Management – NBIM – has, unlike other active administrators, not a concession to provide active management. NBIM must also be required to provide active management and be subject to independent, competent supervision by the credit supervision. The central party is opposed to the fact that the oil fund, as a long-term investor, is able to sell shares that are not owned and to make decisions on interest rate developments in order to achieve a higher return. The centre party considers that, with its time horizon for current investment areas, the fund should only engage in ordinary share transactions and bond purchases. The financial management of the pension wealth needs to be reviewed and aligned on principles that reduce the risk of loss. The Ministry of Finance will follow up next year’s call for Staten pension fund – Foreign to become a separate management organisation. And we're very happy about that. I just want to stress that in the regulation that comes from the Ministry of Finance, it has to be taken into account that it has to be an active administration, but that it is tightening up the legislation with less risk and more government bonds. That's why it's central that you get such a review. We are also pleased that the Minister of Finance will assess whether the management of the oil fund can be organised in the same way as the People's Security Fund, regardless of the central bank, with its own government appointed by the King or the Stortinget. As regards Document No: 8:69 in 2008-2009 from the Left, I just want to say that the Central Party shares that proposal’s strong emphasis on the need for independent revision of the management of the State pension fund.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e792	This is the third time that the Storting treats a separate report on the State pension fund, or what is called the oil fund in the public mouth. Like most of the others in the Chamber, I would like to thank the rapporteur for a good job. I think it's incredibly important that you have both dialogue and development of these issues. What I had hoped was to be the main interest around during the processing of the paper on the State pension fund for 2008, was the work we've been doing on the ethical guidelines, because until the financial crisis really got under way, that's what the Storting and Government had planned to be the main headline. So let me start there, so I can be absolutely sure that I'm going to go a little bit through some main headings that have been very important. We've had a very broad hearing. Both nationally and internationally, there has been a high level of involvement in the ethical guidelines of the Pension Fund. There is a lot of attention, and there is a lot of emphasis on, for example: the documentation provided by the Ethics Council when recommending extractions and also on the active ownership exercise carried out by Norway’s Bank in these issues. What we are doing now in relation to further developing the ethical guidelines is important and important. We now make it clear that we don't want to invest in companies that produce tobacco. There has been a relatively long discussion about whether this should be included or not, and now comes to the conclusion of a nearly total expansion, i.e. minus the Progress Party and the Right, with an extension in relation to the ethical guidelines here. In addition, very exciting work is being done on the environmental front and in relation to emerging markets. One is that we warn that we want an investment programme in relation to environmental investment. We're getting closer to how this is going to be done in the national budget, and I see there's a broad consensus in the Stortingen on that. In addition, the notification airs to do the same with respect to investments in emerging markets. There's no clear conclusion from the Government's side, but I also see the commitment that has been in the committee around this, and I take it with me in the further work. Then we have notified that we want Norway's Bank to develop so-called expectation documents in at least two new areas. One is about the environment and climate. It's about what kind of expectations you present on behalf of the fund in relation to the companies we're invested in. But it's not just about the environment and the climate, it's also about transparency and it's going to be, what to say, a way to complement the work we're now doing internationally to fight tax havens and other opportunities to hide ownership structures, for example. It is important and essential for the work on the Ethical Guidelines of the Fund. In addition, we establish an observation list to be a sort of intermediate point between extracts, or recommendations on extracts from the Ethics Council, and the active ownership exercise from the Bank of Norway. We have already put a company, namely Siemens, on such an observation list, and we believe that we can have a greater impact on cooperation in view of the different instruments we have in relation to the ethical work on the fund. A few–three days ago, I also announced that we should follow-up work on the impact of climate change on financial markets, and we have now concluded an agreement with a separate company called Mercer to do just that – at a general level, but also on what kind of advice we as a fund can get in that context. So in a lot of areas here, we have now developed the work done on the ethical guidelines, and especially in the field of environment and development, the focus is much stronger. Then to the turbulent year 2008. So the Pension Fund is Europe's largest fund, and one of the world's absolute largest funds. When we have chosen an investment strategy that largely links the development of the fund to the development of world markets, it is quite obvious and no surprise that the values of the fund are affected by what is happening, when we have the greatest fall in share prices of 100 years and the strongest economic crisis since World War II. We have had a significant reduction in the fund's values, and when the world's stock markets fall by 40 p.m., when Oslo Børs falls by 50 p.m., when the listed property companies fall by 60 p.m., it is obvious that our fund also experiences a decrease in value – 23 p.m. for the rest of the world and 25 pst. for the Social Security Fund, which is the investment in Norway. But our goal of risk for the fund is not to have the least possible variation over time. We're a big fund, with a very long investment horizon, and it was Stortinget's very clear consciousness when we discussed what kind of risk spread we should have on the fund, if we were to increase the share from 40 to 60 pst. Everyone who participated in that discussion had a very long-term horizon in relation to what kind of decision we were going to make, and the question of correct timing for that decision was not what was emphasised by Stortinget either. I also understand that the public debate about the Pension Fund has been, what to say, much better prepared to assess whether it was wise or not to go from an increase in the share from 40 to 60 p.m., because we had discussed this in detail. Everyone knew that the judgment, whether it was wise or not, could not be dismissed in a year. And we might think that when we look back and decide whether the timing, which we haven't put the emphasis on, was wise or not, we'll see that it's also coming out very favorable in the light of history, because you haven't ever got as many shares for a barrel of oil as in 2008. Since we have such a long investment horizon, the timing can also go up from 40 to 60 pst. in retrospect, it is said to have been quite wise. But we don't get the verdict of history now, we can't get it either now or in a few years. A little visit to the Progress Party in this context: I think the Advance Party is quite confusing in expressing their investment strategy and risk profile for the Fund. First, they think you need to invest 10 pst. on property. That there should be a low risk profile, in a situation where not least the property market has been a huge bubble and lost more than any other market, I think there are good reasons to put a big question mark on it. Secondly, I think you are very approximate in the discussion about what the impairment is due to. For example, an impairment of over DKK 600 billion associated with the Pension Fund is not about a purchase from 40 to 50 pst. the share of the shares, as expressed by the Advance Party. What I think there is a completely different need for proper anchoring of the future, and which we are now going to work hard to present to the Stortinge in a big message in a year's time, is the reason for having active management. There we do not have satisfactory results for 2008. In reality, there is now index management of the interest rate papers, active management of the shares, which, after all, has proved to be positive in the time that has passed. But we have been actively managing the State pension fund since 1998 without any real, thorough debate as to why we have it, what extent it should have, and what kind of requirements you should have for risk management. That debate, I believe, is absolutely necessary for the Stortinget to resume. The same applies to the issue of who receives the management mandate on behalf of the Stortinget and the Ministry of Finance. We've been very pleased that the Norwegian Bank has that mission, and I think it's very strong reasons for them to continue to have it, but it's also reasonable to explain, as the committee is about, the benefits and disadvantages that are at it. This is a very important and important debate. It's healthy with all the debates around the State pension fund, because it's about the people's savings. There are people who are going to have insight and trust that we are really managing our savings.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e1066	This proposal by the Progress Party refers to today's recession, and it is considered to be an argument for improving the conditions for entrepreneurs. We have had similar proposals for treatment earlier in the period, but with a more general justification. For example, it was considered to establish an industrial deduction for self-employed workers, in line with employees' minimum deductions, as late as 29 April last year, when we dealt with a Document No. Eight proposals from the representatives of the Left. As you can see from the setting, the evaluations from the different batches this year are the same as last year. The proposalers consider that it is appropriate to consider setting up a scheme in which the operator of an individual enterprise may choose between a minimum deduction equal to the minimum deduction for ordinary employees and the deduction of actual expenditure, or alternatively it should be considered to introduce a scheme in which individual enterprises are granted an economic deduction equivalent to the tax relief contained in the minimum deduction for ordinary employees. In the case of tax deductions for pensions, the proposers believe that individual enterprises should have the same right to deduct as other employees of an enterprise. They also believe that one should consider whether individual enterprises and employees should not be treated in the same way in terms of social rights such as sickness benefits, unemployment benefits and pay guarantee schemes. The setting contains several suggestions from the opposition parties, and I expect them to explain them themselves. The majority of the Committee, composed of the representatives of the Workers' Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Centre, argue that the structure of a well-designed tax system should not be altered by the economic cycle. The government has stressed that tax measures resulting from the short-term economic survey should be targeted and adapted to the extent of the economic downturn we are now facing. The same majority share the view of the Minister of Finance that the economic situation does not provide a basis for a re-examination of the issues raised in this proposal for a representative. Otherwise, we show that the current government has implemented several changes in this period to improve the conditions of self-employed workers, for example. entitlement to 100 pst pregnant and parental leave. coverage, up to 6 G, and that the tax deduction for pension contributions was doubled in 2006, from 2 pst. to 4 pst. With regard to the proposal to apply an industrial deduction scheme to self-employed workers, our assessments are the same as one year ago, namely that the minimum deduction for employees is a deduction from private costs for the acquisition of income in general income. Self-employed persons are deducted from the actual costs of entrepreneurial income, so that net entrepreneurial income is included in general income. The majority believe that if a person wishes to provide relief to traders by increasing the deductions in general income, a choice should be made to increase the deduction of the person rather than the minimum deduction. The rationale for this is the distributive effect, by providing a progressive income tax through such deductions, so that those with low incomes receive the lowest tax rate of total income. For the concepts of greater non-discrimination in social benefits, such as: health benefits, unemployment benefits and pay guarantee schemes, we show that there is a great difference today between the contributions to the financing of these schemes. If a person wishes to change these conditions, it is necessary to calculate the costs. Increased fees will obviously be necessary if the rights of this group are extended in relation to today. For the whole of this group, in that case, expenditure increases imposed on compensation for the current voluntary supplementary insurance system will be taken into account. Finally, I would like to point out that improvements in social security rules have a cost. Such changes therefore need to be assessed against other proposals and their costs. We believe that such assessments are part of an ordinary budgetary process. There is no disagreement on Stortinget that entrepreneurs are welcome and wanted – even what they get. So how are things in Norway? Innovation Norway announced on 7 May this year that Norway was close to the founding head in 2008. The figures were found in the GEM report, from the Global Entrepreneurship Monitor, and led to a look-up in Today’s Business with the title ‘Founders on the Advancement’. Overall, Norway ranks fifth among the countries surveyed. Denmark is, for example, in the 16th place. Norway is particularly prominent in the use of new technologies. We have Europe’s highest share, only after Ireland, of early-stage entrepreneurship with a potential for 20 or more jobs. As you can see, the framework conditions for this type of activity in Norway are not bad. With the reasons I have given earlier in my speech, on behalf of the majority parties, I would recommend that the proposals in Document No. 8:74 is not acceptable.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e1089	Norway is now in an economic downturn. It's a global crisis. And if there's one group of people who can get Norway out of the crisis, it's founders with good ideas. It's just looking at history in the last few hundred years -- how societies have gotten wealth and growth. It is then important that we have legislation that supports entrepreneurs, does not prevent entrepreneurs. What we in the Progress Party want with this proposal for a representative ‘on better rules for sole proprietors and entrepreneurs operating individual enterprises’ is that they must be respected and treated under the same conditions as employees receive today. It's not like that. The majority of entrepreneurs start on their own with the form of an individual enterprise – especially because it is easy to start, there are few start-up costs and there are other benefits, such as: the discharge of costs to the auditor. Unfortunately, many disadvantages are also: There is a very high risk, and one alone has full responsibility for the company's obligations. Activities of individual enterprises do not provide unemployment benefits for owners. Individual enterprises provide a basis for sickness benefits from popular security, but only 65 pst. from Day 17. Individual-person enterprises do not grant the owner rights equivalent to those which the operator would have obtained if he established a limited liability company or a Norwegian-registered Foreign Enterprise, so-called NUF. The business of an individual enterprise will also miss other benefits that ordinary employees have, such as: minimum deductions on the declaration of pay-guarantee scheme tax-free treatment insurance duty-free continuing training entitlement to care allowance for sick children 100 pst. care allowance coverage and training money coverage We can certainly say that women are more affected than men, with a greater proportion of women than men choosing to start individual enterprises rather than other forms of organisation. All the members of the Financial Committee have received a letter from the Confederation of Enterprises. You would think that the individual parties had taken care of reading it. They support and confirm what we in the Progress Party address in our proposal. The company association has also calculated how much a founder loses on this. If an entrepreneur operates an individual enterprise and has an entrepreneurial income of DKK 400000, he pays a whole of NOK 32500 more in taxes than an employee with the same income. In addition, there is a higher social security charge. The company also comments on the letter of reply from Minister of Finance Kristin Halvorsen to Stortinget dated 12 May 2009, from which I would like to read. The Minister of Finance argues against the minimum deduction for traders with the following: «Activists are deducted from the actual costs of entrepreneurial income, so that net entrepreneurial income is included in general income». The association then answers: ‘Unfortunately, the argument is not sufficient for the actual conditions. For example, those who are self-employed may: a maximum deduction of 6,000 km per car used in industry per year, although they can prove a multiple driving distance and have only a standard amount of 1500 kroner per year for home offices. In a number of other areas, too, there are very limited opportunities for costing costs related to industry.’» The Association of Norwegian Accounting Officers (NARF) also fights for better conditions for sole proprietors. It's a union you should listen to. An investigation they carried out for a period of time showed that self-employed persons in individual enterprises are penalised by up to NOK 40000 at additional costs compared to those in employment. In these times of crisis, there is a lot of talk about investing in research, but for entrepreneurs with good ideas that run individual enterprises, there are other rules. The self-employment of traders is not included in the costs that form the basis for tax deductions under the Tax FUNN scheme. By comparison, the same person receives a tax deduction if he organises the operation as a limited liability company and pays a salary to himself. There are about 20 000 NUFs, i.e. Norwegian registered Foreign Enterprises, registered in the Brønn Island Health Registers now. Is there a development of government parties proud of? The rapporteur said that it is very good that is happening in Norway, and that is what it is. Many new companies have been established in recent years. But they are established because of the tax changes that have forced people to start corporations. Then there have actually been several bankruptcies. In conclusion, I address the proposals we are promoting with others and alone. I would also like to point out that we support proposal No. 5, promoted by the Right and Christian People's Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e1179	Document No 8:74 from the Progress Party on better rules for entrepreneurs and entrepreneurs operating individual enterprises is a very important topic. Some of the background to the document is that, under current tax rules, it is economically advantageous to organise its limited financial liability business, such as limited liability companies, rather than as individual enterprises, where the owner and the performer have unlimited financial liability. Should there be a tax difference, the difference should be in the favour of the sea-going trader because of the greatest risk there. I want to start with, as the rapporteur was in the case, that there have been two important things in this period of government, two important things that the red-green government got into. The first was in 2008, when the entitlement of the sea-going sole proprietor to maternity and maternity benefits was increased to 100 p.p. coverage up to 6 G, while increasing the social security charge to cover the additional costs of the measure. Furthermore, in 2006 the right to deduct the contribution pension was doubled from 2 to 4 pst. It is an important matter that is well received. This government prioritised it among other priority measures and got through to it, and we in the Center Party are very pleased about that. The first point of the proposal concerns the deductions from activity of individual enterprises. We have minimum deductions for employees, and we have personal deductions – or what is called class deductions. The deduction from general income is granted regardless of whether the income is wages, salaries, capital, pensions or entrepreneurial income. These types of deductions result in progressive income taxation, so that those with low income receive the lowest tax rate as a percentage of total income. The deduction has increased from 1992 from NOK 21 700 to NOK 38 850 in 2008. It is a strong increase, but much lower than the minimum deduction. If a person wishes to provide relief also to traders by increasing the deduction in general income, a choice should be made to increase the deduction of the person rather than the minimum deduction. In order to ensure equal treatment between employees and seafarers, it should be considered that a higher deduction should be made from the general income of the enterprise. As mentioned above, the minimum deduction has increased significantly in the last year. A corresponding increase in the amount of the person deduction has not been made. This therefore increases the need to assess a higher deduction from the entrepreneurial income of maritime operators. That assessment is precisely to establish that the taxability principle must apply to maritime operators as to employees. So to the next point in the document, the proposal of social rights. Contribute to the financing of popular security through, inter alia: the social security contributions are different for employees and traders. Benefits from social security for employees and different maritime workers are very different, where employees have the highest social security contributions. The members of the Committee of the Governments of the Parties say that it would be reasonable to consider increased fees for maritime operators in order to extend the rights of that group in the area of public security. The tax deduction on the contribution pension for traders was increased from 2 to 4 pst. in 2006. The short period since that increase makes it difficult to assess the need and consequences of any change in this rate. The work of increasing minimum deductions and improving social security rights for maritime traders is prioritised by the Centre Party more than the improvement of tax deductions for deposit pensions. Finally, a very important theme is the fiscal and social framework for maritime traders versus employees. Most entrepreneurs start out as self-employed workers, many times through part-time work, to try out something other than paid work from other employers. Greater safety as a sea-going operator, including: In the case of disease, it is important to stimulate more people to step into the ranks of entrepreneurs – which is something that all parties are talking positively about. But it's demanding. It requires greater equity, greater saving, greater long-term sustainability. It is a very different culture to go into the ranks of the maritime traders. Therefore, it is very central that the framework conditions are facilitated. The centre party is now very concerned with concrete proposals for improvements. For us, it is important that there be under two safe slogans: Do your duty, demand your right – and tax by ability.

At my discretion, there is an important point here that the Government addresses to a very small extent. I think it would be vulnerable to Norway as a small nation to become the largest user of a version of a fighter plane. It's an incentive contract, and all of the partners that buy the same configuration in the same year are going to have the same price. There is no element of ‘wheeling and selling’ in this price, and I think it is reassuring. But here's an important problem issue that could probably have been explained better than the proposition put up. The third element, and it cannot turn around the purely professional assessment, is that it is important that such a large investment also benefits Norwegian jobs. This contract is a different design from the traditional buy-back design, the so-called best value principle, where there is no guarantee that we will get contracts, but if you are the best and cheapest, you also have great opportunities. This is probably also a system that is socio-economically better than the traditional buy-back system. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e1333	In recent months, the Defence Committee has carried out a comprehensive treatment of the largest investment in defence, namely the purchase of new fighter aircraft. The old F-16 planes are getting worn out. Now it's time to think about replacing them. Norway must also be able to establish a contribution of the size of a squadron according to NATO requirements and standards. For the Workers' Party, it's important that the planes meet the defence needs, and so does the JSF. I want to commend the Council of State which has worked very hard since the government change in 2005 to facilitate real competition between the candidates concerned. I therefore note with satisfaction that the two offers we received last year were far better both in terms of content, price and industrial aspects than the case was just three years ago. In short, the competition has worked. I have always been concerned that an investment in this size should also provide good opportunities for the Norwegian industry. We must therefore have a special focus on industrial opportunities in the future. The assessments made in the selection of aeroplanes are not about ‘military requirements versus industry’, which some people seem to believe. We had already stated in the specifications that the military requirements and price should be superior to the industrial conditions. Because of course, it is not appropriate to buy a new plane that is clearly worse than the competitor, even if we were to get some jobs in exchange. The Battle Light Purchase is primarily about buying a fighter plane for the Defense. At the same time, this is a major national effort that will also benefit our industry. As long as repurchase — or ‘best value’ used in this case — is used in most countries for defence investment, Norway must also make use of it. In the vote, the majority will make clear demands for the negotiators. Therefore, I also feel a special need to say something about the conditions of the industry associated with such a large investment as this is. It is important that the purchase of aircraft produces significant industrial effects. Norway has extensive experience in repurchasing and industrial cooperation to help position the Norwegian defence-related industry in the international arena. This is the case with the F-16 purchase 35 years ago, this is the case with the frigatan acquisition and also with the transport aircraft that we have recently invested in. The size has tightened the requirements of those negotiating with Lockheed Martin on the industrial agreements during the course of the investigation. The Labour Party believes that in cooperation with the Norwegian industry we must be able to demand special attention in order to further develop the industrial plan that Lockheed Martin has already presented. I have no doubt that the government will have a special focus on the industrial opportunities associated with the chosen candidate during the contract negotiations. I trust that they will ensure that the follow-up of the industrial strategy is given high priority. It is important for us that possible deliveries to the aircraft, not least integration of Norwegian developed systems, are followed up in particular. American Lockheed Martin builds its industrial agreements that are known by what is called the best value, and not by repurchase. In other words, industrial participation in the JSF programme is based on the principle that the industrial and research and development environments of the participating nations will be given the opportunity to compete for direct missions to the development and production of the aeroplanes. That is, we cannot demand a repurchase. On the other hand, it provides significant opportunities for Norwegian suppliers that are competitive on quality and price. I believe that Norwegian industry is to the highest degree. Among other things, Kongsberg, Nammo and others, with their hundreds of subcontractors, has shown it for a long time. I believe that based on the needs of the defence, the industrial policy aspects of the acquisition of fighter aircraft will contribute to increased national value creation and development of a competitive business, as well as ensuring the defence of the necessary availability of competence, material and services in the future. The decision today is a long step towards a new fighter aircraft for Norway. It shall replace the wearer F-16, which is gradually becoming both worn and operated. Now let's make a drop-down. It's still a few years before we get the planes. It's important to make the right choices at the right time. It gives us advantages and opportunities that we might otherwise lose. I believe that what we do by making a choice today is right. Now we're well equipped to negotiate with Lockheed Martin, and we can buy the planes at the most favourable time in relation to, among other things. price. Now we're sitting with the good cards, and we're going to take advantage of them. Finally, I would also like to thank the chairman of the committee and the rapporteur, Mr Petersen, for his solid efforts to put in place a good attitude from the committee, which I can certainly recommend to the Stortinget to join.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e1543	Unfortunately, the centre party is in the situation that we have not had a member of the committee. It was adopted early in 2001 to prepare for the creation of new fighter aircraft. Then there were three current candidates: Eurofighter, JAS Gripen and F-35 Joint Strike Fighter. For a while, it seemed that the process could actually end with just one candidate, but two candidates – Eurofighter and JSF – were successfully put in place for different reasons. With the crucial role fighter aircraft play in our defence structure, it is necessary to meet the operational requirements in the best possible way. Extensive quality assessments have been carried out and follow-up questions have been raised by the operators. An operational performance review and an assessment of the operational impact of candidates on different assignments have also been carried out. The centre party has stressed that only the JSF meets the Norwegian operational and technical requirements. External quality insurers support this assessment. That is the background for starting a negotiation process in order to acquire this new type of fighter aeroplane. The government has assessed it in such a way that in some specific areas the candidate Gripen NG does not meet the Norwegian requirements set for technical performance, etc. As a result, Gripen became a second choice in the competition, and is no longer a current candidate. It is especially in the areas of sensor capacity, opportunities to remain undiscovered and electronic warfare that the JSF meets the Norwegian requirements better than the Gripen. It's a message that we just get to take note of. It has been important for the Centre Party to be able to establish that the fighting aircraft process has been a process that has been strictly regulated and formal, in order to maintain the competitive aspect in line with ‘best practice’ in the procurement area. Competitors have been evaluated in a thorough, comparable and verifiable manner. The project has been subject to external quality assurance in line with the applicable provisions, process and methods in this field, and we note the statement that the flight flight process has been carried out in a professionally and ethically sound manner.
