I would like to stress that I do not see it as a coincidence, but as a good thing that we still have American pre-storers in Trøndelag, there are significant ally aircraft operations, for example. around the Ørland and that NATO’s AWACS operations are still taking place from Norwegian airports. It doesn't happen because it's the strategic image, but it happens because we're acting in Norway in a proper way. Relationships are built that go beyond the formal ones, and they are of value. So that way, the left is all satisfied with what we've done. And I would like to remind you of the following, since I have taken a Nordic perspective: I have traveled and studied the northern regions in recent years on behalf of the Stortinget. The Finns made it quite clear, in meeting them, when I visited Helsinki last year, that they had the choice between the JAS Gripen and the F-18 Hornet, and they chose the F-18 Hornet. And they made it very clear that that's how they chose. They did not find at any time that it would mean anything non-Nordic. On the contrary, they were actually more concerned, in clear terms, about the defence capacity of our neighbouring country Sweden than they were about defence development in Norway. And Finns can at least be attributed to the notion that they have some hard realism behind a lot of their experiences. They are expressed in a scarce language, so you have to understand the Finnish language, translated into Norwegian, to understand what they are saying. But that's in sum what they say and what they don't say, so you have a lot to learn. So they understood why they had to renew their fighter aircraft. In the opinion of the left, it's also the way we should do it. And then it's essential that the chosen aircraft satisfy both international and national scenarios. The national scenarios have to do with our sovereignty in our difficult coastal areas and in our northern regions. So that's clearly important. I have also noticed that the chairperson of the defense has paid great attention to the fact that we are going to have a fighter aircraft. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e1607	On 19 December last year, the government presented the proposal for new fighter aircraft to the Defence. This was entirely according to the timetable that we had openly communicated for several years. The proposal proposes the launch of a negotiation process for the acquisition of F-35 Joint Strike Fighter. That is what we are discussing today. I would like to stress the importance of the election of a candidate now. There's actually no upside, or anything to profit from waiting with this decision. The selection should take place so that we can continue our participation in F-35 with renewed intensity. A devaluation now makes it possible to be an active and credible partner country. Norway is the first country outside the United States to actually choose F-35. At the same time, when it is several years until we finally undertake to buy the plane, Norway will be in a positive position to promote Norwegian-developed solutions. This is beneficial both for Norwegian industrial participation and for the specific operational needs of Norway for adaptations on the aircraft itself. A postponement of the elections would bring Norwegian industrial policy opportunities back a long way. I am therefore pleased that a majority in the Defence Committee is in favour of the launch of a negotiation process for the acquisition of new fighter aircraft of type F-35. In such a big case, and in a case that will go over so long, I also fully agree with the case rapporteur that it is of great importance that there is a broad majority behind this. That is why I am also pleased that both the Christian People's Party and the Left have signalled that they support the majority in the matter. I am also surprised to note that the Advance Party is opposed to the choice of F-35, and at the same time clearly states that you are concerned that the Defense is getting the most suitable fighter aircraft to perform the tasks. In addition, it is stated that military needs are essential for the choice of fighter aircraft. But that's exactly what's the main reason for the Government's choice of F-35. I also record that the party uses concrete texts from the central supporting documents of the Government in the fighting aircraft case as a “proof” for our mistakes. The government is asked to ‘present a much better, more thorough and more sound decision-making basis for the Storting, where the two options have been assessed against each other cost-effectively and militarily’. Once again, that's exactly what the government has done. I would like to stress that since this government took over the autumn of 2005, we have had a strong focus on establishing a genuine and credible competition between the candidates, with the aim of finding the best fighter aircraft for Norway. I therefore do not understand the many concerns of the Advance Party in the fighting aircraft case. Candidates have been treated equally throughout the process. The various milestones and decision-making times have been openly communicated, and the competitive cycle has been fully implemented in line with this. The Ministry of Defence has also been aware of the criteria underlying the choice of fighter aircraft, and these requirements have been communicated in detail to both candidates. It is probably the suppliers themselves who have to respond to whether they can meet these requirements. Until the decision to opt for F-35 was made, both of the remaining candidates expressed that they felt that competition was real and that the process was clean. In order to assess the contestants in a thorough, comparable, fair and verifiable manner, the Ministry of Defence sent out in January 2008 a request for binding information to the three respective authorities’ organisations. Both the Swedish and US authorities answered the request by the deadline of 28 April 2008, while the German authorities chose not to answer the request. This was their own choice. The request contained all detailed requirements and questions that are considered relevant in relation to satisfying Norwegian ambitions for a future fighter aircraft. Central was the need for information on candidates’ operational characteristics, the possibility of multinational cooperation throughout the life of the aeroplane, cost, lifetime costs and industrial opportunities. The request also contained specific descriptions of the various threats to combat refugees would be measured against. So it was perfectly clear to the candidates what ‘examination task’ they were going to do. It was also a basic premise for the process that the fighter aircraft should have an ability to operate autonomously in response to a potential enemy threat – or independently, as the case-responsible rapporteur said. In a defence model such as the Norwegian one, this is particularly important. We can't always draw on the combined breadth of alternative capabilities that a larger country's more differentiated defence could do. The more important it becomes that each platform has significant autonomy within all the main categories of air force. In addition, fighter aircraft must be able to participate as contributors, together with other resources, in the solution of different tasks. This emphasis on the autonomy of each platform is a key point in today’s network-based defence. It's amazing to me that the Progress Party in its comments chooses to attack this very central assumption. The scenarios used in the evaluation are the same as those underlying the Defence Study 07 and the Government's work on the long-term proposal for the Defence. The various analyses and assessments of the project are based on the respective responses of the operators. Where there were uncertainties in the answer, they sent clarifying questions to the providers. Before offering their answers, they also had the opportunity to ask questions about the fighter aircraft project. Both operators avail themselves of this opportunity, both in writing and through meeting activities. The Kampfly project then evaluated the responses of the operators along three main axes: achievement of objectives in relation to the technical and operational requirements – the so-called military professional assessment – costs and industrial opportunities. In the military field, three different types of assessments have been carried out: assessment of the individual requirements — and here it was about. thousands of requirements – operational efficiency analysis in data models and assessment of the operational impact of candidates on different missions. All three independent assessments in the military field show that F-35 is the only candidate who fully meets the Norwegian technical and operational requirements set for the future fighter aircraft. In addition, F-35 had lower acquisition and lifetime costs than the JAS Gripen. In the light of the comments of the Progress Party, I also find it necessary to mention that a number of meetings have been carried out with the Swedish authorities since the Government's decision was published on 20 November last year. Among other things, there have been meetings with the Swedish Defence Minister, the Swedish Defence Chief, the Air Force Chief and the Swedish Defence Materielverk. Through these meetings, the Swedish delegations got a deep insight into our analytical methods and results, up to a secret level. There have also been meetings with the Swedish supplier Saab. As indicated in the established large-scale state investment legislation, which has had the approval of different governments in the last ten years, external quality assurances have been subject to the entire process. Of course, it has been in agreement with the Ministry of Finance. I am very pleased that these people agree with the professional recommendations, and that they have concluded that the process has been carried out in a professional and ethically sound manner. So I feel completely secure about the professional work that has been the basis for the Government's decision before Christmas last year. The fight lighting process has been strictly regulated and formal – this is precisely in line with ‘best practice’ in the area of procurement. Just one comment on this with currency: it's not like we've based a dollar rate on a given date. The dollar rate used in the proposition is NOK Sixty-four. It's an average calculated dollar rate, an average calculated in advance, for the years we're going to acquire these planes. So it's not a random date when the offer was made. Then I would like to say a few words about the case proposal, because we have been criticised for the fact that the proposition is too thin. At least we have thought that we have reasoned and explained the election and asked for the negotiating authorisation further. It is important to clarify that this is not an economic negotiator. We'll get back to 2011. Much of the basic documentation was published, placed online, at the moment the proposition was sent to Stortinget. In addition, we have provided a lot of the basic documentation here. I think it is very positive that the committee has spent a lot of time getting involved in this large case complex.

I am confident that the further negotiations will show that this will have major ring-fences for the Norwegian industry, but I also have great expectations that the government in the forthcoming negotiations will be fully pressurised to make the industrial package as good as possible. In this way, we can acquire the world's most modern aircraft, while having a lot of industrial activity in our country. I would encourage everyone who believes that there is no difference between political parties and politicians in general, to take a trip to Kongsberg. Here we both see and believe that the differences are very large. I am proud to join in choosing a candidate for negotiation, primarily because we get an aircraft that is suitable for caring for Norwegian interests in a new time, but also because the acquisition is likely to provide Norwegian industry with adventurous opportunities in the future.

We see clear security policy implications in turning the defense's capabilities into a combat aircraft capacity that should be able to operate autonomously inwardly over strong air-monitored and protected hostile areas. This has been mentioned many times, but for us it is a very important matter. The Progress Party wants Norwegian defence and security policy to be anchored in an overall increase, with the emphasis on continued cooperation and good relations with our neighbours. On the existing basis, the Party of Progress does not find sufficient substance to evaluate the two operators against each other. The case needs to be elaborated more thoroughly, systematised in matrix format, so that all aspects of the offer can be assessed more easily. This review can only be complete in close cooperation with the two providers. St. pp. No 36 should not be taken into account now, but sent back to the Government for a more thorough decision-making basis, until it returns to the Stortinget.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e2019	The cultural activities of Defence are an important part of the culture of society in general. In-house culture plays an important role, and this communication seeks to make defence needs and special traditions visible. I believe that the message does well, which the entire committee agrees to. To this end, the Committee has few comments in addition to its being associated with the notification. The main elements of the message – and which the message takes into account and deepens – are the fastening works, the museums, the music and the landmarks of war. Furthermore, it is argued that the defence sector itself organises and manages cultural activities and that the Ministry of Defence is responsible. In order to maintain the culture of Defence, it is necessary to have a long-term time horizon. Therefore, this message ranges from the current day to approx. 2020. But it's not enough with just beautiful words and phrases. A clear pre-condition for the notification to become a reality is that it is followed up in the annual budgets. In order to make this more serious, the Committee notes that the facts of the notification are expected to be «follow-up» in the annual budgets and not merely that they will be followed up. The economy in this must be a clear assumption. If not, this – as I say initially – is just words and phrases. There is no disagreement about this. The recently adopted long-term plan refers, inter alia, to: that the music of the Defence has had a central function as a tradition bearer and history manager, and that the national fortifications have a meaning as national cultural memories. In addition to the fact that this has also been attempted to be visible through this message, one also – in a good way, I would say – tries to do the same with the museum and the war memorials. Today we have 14 national fortifications – from Vardøhus in the north to Fredriksten in the south. They all have different stories and characters, and they represent a part of the history of Defence for more than 800 years. Three of the attachments – Akershus, Bergenhus and Karljohansverm – are still in active military use. With the exception of these, the defense has stepped down the activity of the attachments and has made them more accessible to the public. In other words, they have gone from fighting facilities to cultural memories. It's Defence Buildings that manage the fortifications, and it's a pleasure that the commandship has been reintroduced on part of the fortifications. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e2199	Today we're going to deal with two things about veterans. A veteran is one who sets up when the world community asks for it. A veteran is willing to risk life and health for others to live in peace and freedom. A veteran is one who deserves all the honor and recognition we can give. A veteran is one we all have to be proud of. That's why we're also going to take care of our veterans. We as a nation must and must set up for the veterans when they need it, just as they have set up for us. Today is therefore an important day both for the veterans and for us when we are to adopt St.meld. No 34 for 2008-2009, «From Protective to Veterinary» On the supervision of personnel before, during and after participation in foreign operations, and when to adopt Ot.prp later this evening. No 67 for 2008-2009, on strengthening the rights of veterans following international operations. I want to get into both cases now. Today’s decisions will strengthen the oversight of the personnel we send out in foreign operations and help them to get well-deserved recognition for their efforts. Participation in foreign operations is an important element of Norwegian security and defence policy and is at present a central part of the defence activities. When we send out women and men on risky missions in war-torn and conflict-torn areas, it is the great responsibility of the Storting and society to take care of them both before, during and after the mission. About 120,000 women and men have been involved in foreign operations on behalf of Norway since World War II. These personnel have made a great effort to create peace and stability out there and security at home. A prerequisite for maintaining personnel is that the operations in which we participate have a solid grounding in international law and are perceived as legitimate. The government will therefore continue to be a driver internationally in order to create robust mandates and clear rules for the use of power. The vast majority of soldiers feel that they come home with enhanced competence and positive experience after completing a meaningful service. At the same time, we know that participation in the operations poses a risk that some people may suffer both physical and mental damage. In order to better prevent and take care of those who are injured, we rely on more knowledge, especially on psychological damage. I am therefore pleased that the Government will prioritise research in this area, with emphasis on scale and causality, and on how such damage can best be prevented and followed up. Recruitment of personnel involved in foreign operations is a responsibility affecting several sectors of society. Because the veterans have different needs, the defence must work closely with, among other things. the Civil Health Service and the Staff Regulations of Employment and Welfare. It is particularly important that competences are transferred from defence to the other social sectors. We therefore need to focus more on training and more on interaction. It is also important to follow up the families of those who are out. Veterinary and personnel organisations play a very important role in improving staff and veterans’ care. I would argue that it is therefore essential to succeed in continuing the good cooperation with the veteran and employee organisations on the further development in this area. In this case, the Council of State deserves commendation. Ever since she joined, she has been working to implement measures to give our veterans increased recognition, strengthened rights and a good and worthy offer. More resources are provided in the field of science, through several annual works, increased appropriations and new family offerings, and so-called low-threshold schemes such as meeting places and peer support schemes for veterans are established. With Ot.prp. No 67 for 2008-2009, we will make it easier for veterans to get compensation for damage they have suffered in international operations. The veterans get a good compensation system. The state includes this is clearly responsible for the women and men who are going into international operations in the future, but at the same time we are clearing up the old cases. In order to strengthen the rights of veterans, an objective liability for personal injury is legalised, limited up to 65 G. The State shall, independently of guilt, make good any loss suffered by military and civil personnel in the Defence for damage or disease caused by service, including mental damage. At the same time, we've been very concerned about solving the veterinary issues that are far back in time. Some things are over 30 years old. These are mainly due to psychological damage. For those who have suffered psychological damage in the period from 1978 until the law enters into force, we will introduce a separate compensation scheme. The specific compensation scheme is limited up to 35 G, which is 2.45 million. kr. It is not common in Norwegian law to give retroactive effect to new laws. But we in the Labour Party believe that for us it is absolutely necessary that we now also resolve the old issues. There is also a right to psychiatric and psychological follow-up from the Defence for staff who serve and who have served, in international operations, for one year after the end of service. It is an important social task to take care of those who have given years of their lives for the service of peace. When society asks Norwegian women and men to risk life and health for peace and freedom — often far away from Norway — yes, society must also stand up when they come home. The government has put the veto case on the agenda, and I mean with my hand on the heart that we have got a lot already. We've now got our own veteran center at Bæria by Kongswinger. Here the veterans should be able to come and meet other veterans. We have also launched a number of other actions in close cooperation with the veterinary organisations. The defence has established its own veterinary administration and the head of defence is in the process of creating a separate directive which will underline the responsibility of the defence for the care of the veterans. We have contributed to the development of our own military outpatient clinic, which, in addition to offering its own treatment, also helps veterans up against the public health system. While taking care of those who are struggling, we must remember that the vast majority of the over 100,000 veterans we have in Norway have come home with positive experiences. Our soldiers deserve our consideration and respect. We need to focus on the important job they do. I am proud of our women and men who risk their lives and their health when Norwegian society asks them to do so. Then it's only fair and fair that the Norwegian society stands up for the veterans when they ask for it. The legislative proposals that the Government has now promoted show that the state takes a clear responsibility for its veterans. To St. Report. 34 is a unanimous attitude. In the Ot.prp committee setting. No 67 there are some minority proposals, which I'm assuming the parties that are behind them will prepare.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e2467	St.meld by the Government. No 13 for 2008-2009 on development policy raises many important issues. As the rapporteur for the Committee's position, I am pleased to be able to conclude that here in the Grand Duchy again we have the power to create important, agreed conclusions on most of the major issues, although they are also these. Development assistance is only one part of our interface with developing countries. In order to create a more coherent and comprehensive Norwegian development policy, changes are needed in other policy areas, so that they strongly support the objectives of development policy. Therefore, it is politically important when the entire Committee is united in stating that the premise for closer links between development and foreign policy should be more consistent with the objectives of the Norwegian development policy, which are poverty reduction and the promotion of human rights. It is essential that aid policy be geared to developing needs of developing countries and that it is not made a tool to promote the interests of industrialised countries. Globalisation is increasing the need to finance global assets, such as international security, operating financial systems, a stable climate, security against epidemics, etc. We need a new category of international statistics, so that nations can make their contribution to funding international actions to secure global benefits. It is positive that the Government launches that idea. The Committee goes further. Norway can go ahead and set an example for others in this area. A Joint Committee therefore asks the Government to consider setting up a dedicated budget area for the financing of global common funds, other than the aid budget. The Committee further points out that what is to be covered by the aid budget is actions which have the primary objective of developing poor countries, while actions which have global common resources as their primary objective are to be financed from other budgets. Where the main objective is not development assistance to poor countries, this shall not be financed. Evaluation has shown clear weaknesses in development cooperation. It is our duty to improve the assistance. In order not to exceed the capacity of the assistance manager, the entire Committee considers that a large-scale and long-term bilateral cooperation from state to state should concentrate on fewer countries than is currently the case. There is an international trend towards failure of long-term, inadequate fulfilment of assistance commitments, and rapid shifts in focus and assistance facilities are being made by donor countries. To this end, the Committee clarifies that development cooperation should be based on long-term, professionalism and the needs and assumptions of the recipient countries. The main message mentions far more areas than before as sectors where Norway has valuable experience and competence for use in development cooperation. That's good. But we must always remember that it is the needs of developing countries that must decide on the choice of response. The assistance must be recipient-oriented, non-gender-oriented. The UN's Millennium Goals are the leaders of Norwegian development policy. These objectives were jointly adopted by developing countries and developed countries at the turn of the millennium, and commit to us in particular during the period we are now in the mid-term, i.e. until 2015. To this end, the Committee unanimously confirms that aid for the implementation of the Millennium Development Goals, not least in the field of health and education, should be given priority in Norwegian development cooperation. As is well known, Norway has taken on a special responsibility for contributing to the health-related thousand-year targets. We're still going to follow that up. It is good that the Government regularly flag this offensively, both in Norway and internationally. A particularly important and new clarification is made by the entire Committee. It is that the share of Norwegian education aid should now be increased. This is also new in relation to the big picture and an important policy direction for the years to come. The fight against climate change is a global shared responsibility, but the developed countries have a particular historical responsibility and the largest financial and technological resources to tackle the problems. Industrialised countries must therefore go ahead and demonstrate that environmentally friendly economic growth is possible. Also u-country needs clean energy. It can be achieved by hydropower in some places, but we also need to invest more in solar energy and wind power. Conservation of natural forests is a cost-effective way to avoid emissions of CO2. The whole Committee shares that view and stresses the requirement for third party confirmation or verification. It is necessary to avoid so-called carbon leakage by stopping at most one location leading only to increased at most elsewhere. The 2007 UN Climate Action Plan requires the increased funding of climate action to be ‘new and additional’. If the OECD countries now invest the funding of climate action within the development aid budget framework, climate action will be at the expense of the fight against poverty. It may make it difficult to bring developing countries into a new climate agreement. War and conflict create humanitarian crises and sustain poverty. Economic growth and development seem to be anti-war. In Afghanistan, Sudan and the Middle East, military forces are not enough to create security and stability. A comprehensive approach through assistance and policy is needed to create a better and safer future. Many major acts have allowed their geopolitical interests and military foreign exposures to become dominant also for large parts of civil development assistance. Norway and other like-minded countries, on the other hand, have placed greater emphasis on poverty orientation in the aid facility. Norway has been given the credit for this, and I am pleased with the cross-political agreement to continue this line. We have long used a number of instruments to influence large capital flows, so that developing countries can reap greater benefits from international trade and get more private investment so that growth and employment can be created. It is a strong signal when, from an ethical point of view, the entire Committee considers it appropriate to establish a new investment fund outside the assistance budget, based on a limited proportion of the funds of the State pension fund – abroad. The government is asked to assess this further. Another option, which is now being discussed at full international level, is to create new sources of financing for development by introducing small international levies on selected economic activities. It is demanding to have international impact on this, and it could be exciting to hear Norway continue to work on it in the future, if the Council of State could comment on it. Following the intervention of Aktion deletes the unindebtedness and the youth organisation Changemaker, a unanimous committee calls for a Norwegian debt audit to look for illegitimate debt that should be erased and strengthen the rules for responsible lending. I would like to thank all the fractions of the Foreign Affairs Committee for their cooperation and many more good, coherent formulations and guidelines for policy in this area. This is important for a field where Norway has high ambitions, but where good anchoring is important in Stortinget. Finally, on behalf of the Christian People's Party, I would like to briefly highlight five points, which we consider particularly important. Firstly, the average of increasing the share of Norwegian aid in education aid is a battle cause for us. Now a new trend is being announced. We value that highly. Secondly, the inter-politicisation of increasing Norwegian support for agricultural development and food security is important. This we believe will also make the fight against poverty more effective. Thirdly, the demarcation between development aid and the financing of global goods is, in principle, very important. Fourthly, the fact that new climate measures should be really new and so-called additional to development assistance is a principle that it is important to ensure in the years to come. And last, fifthly, I would like to point out that the Christian People's Party – together with the Progress Party and the Left – has its own proposal where the Stortinget asks the Government to set up its own investment fund for poor developing countries. It is necessary, not least now, to counteract the negative effects of the financial crisis on many of the world's poorest countries. There are areas in the world where the most dangerous days in life are the day you are born, and the day you create life by putting children into the world. While Angola is a nation flowing with oil money, the birthplaces in Luanda are among the worst in the world. We don't want it like that. Gender equality is therefore one of the main areas of action — or the main strands — for Norwegian development policy. This is an area where Norway has a special advantage, where we can make a difference. It concerns assistance for training for girls in vulnerable countries or assistance following natural disasters. At a meeting in the International Forum in Oslo Labour Party last week, Jan Egeland told us that when they came in with emergency aid for the victims of the earthquake in Pakistan, it led, among other things, to the death of the victims. for girls to go to school for the first time in the camps that were set up. It is about securing the female farmers in Africa in the negotiations of the World Health Organisation. It also concerns Security Resolution 1325, to give women the power to help determine the development of post-conflict areas when building peace. And it's about "women can" courses, about schooling girls in the voluntary sector and women politicians and parliamentarians in developing countries. These are areas where we can make a difference. These are comparative advantages — a division of labour between countries and aid actors with the aim of contributing to the achievement of the Millennium Goals: removing poverty, ensuring a change in the structures of power within each country and between poor and rich countries. More people will gain control of their own lives. It is about making aid more strategic, but also looking at aid as just part of development policy. That means a closer connection between, among other things. foreign policy, development policy, security policy and environmental policy. It also involves taking strategic action throughout development policy. A large majority of the Committee, all with the exception of the Progress Party, believes it is important to ensure that the poor countries have political space for action, governance and instruments that have been important to develop our own country into a welfare society. This will include, inter alia: say the right to a welfare and distribution policy and the right to an active enterprise and employment policy, which also means the possibility, for a longer period, to protect sectors and industries under construction. Then it is vital that the financial crisis does not lead to an increase in protectionism in our part of the world and to a reduction in development aid. A dynamic business life is a prerequisite for economic growth and development. The path out of poverty and dependence on foreign aid is going through sustainable economic development. It also requires cooperation between the Community and the market, where the Community's welfare services and industrial development together create values. Measures – as the Committee also stresses – are important to formalise the informal sector. In particular, many women sell goods on the market outside the formal economy. Microcredit is one of the instruments of assistance that can help integrate this market into the official economy. Aid for Trade — providing developing countries with trade competence by developing the necessary infrastructure for agricultural and industrial development — is one of the main areas of Norwegian aid. It must also be seen in connection with our own willingness to open our borders to imports from the countries we have assisted with Aid for Trade. I would like to refer here to the debate on Swaziland. It recognises the importance of civil society in developing countries. At least as important is support for political parties. We do not achieve a well-functioning democracy without strong political parties. Support for political parties has been underestimated in aid for too long. The same applies to active support for parliaments, the very basis for a democratic system and for development. For many years, several of us have visited a country where Members of Parliament have asked for financial support in order to function as elected representatives of the people in a fragile democracy. For this reason, the Committee emphasises the need for democratic support. It also means that we believe that we must contribute to the involvement of parliaments in both bilateral and multilateral aid processes. It is important that this responsibility is not managed by each country's government alone, but is enshrined in real democratic processes, including in parliaments. Therefore, the Nordic model of social development is also one of the areas in which Norway has valuable competence – a comparative advantage in our development policy as well. As part of the process of a more strategic development policy, the Government will focus on using the advantage of each aid channel. In order to increase the overall effectiveness of global assistance, we need to ensure a good division of labour between actors. That's why we give priority to equality, and we have to give priority to education. I would like to point out that the National Assembly of the Workers' Party decided that equality and education should be the main priorities of development policy. As regards the division of labour between aid channels, we also see in some countries, cf. Afghanistan or what happened in Bosnia in its time, that coordination of assistance in individual countries is very important, and that the division of labour between different actors is actually very important in that process. We have recognised competence in many areas. We also have that in terms of climate, in terms of peace building, in terms of human rights, and we have it not least in terms of oil management and good governance. It is not that poverty can be halved by 2015 just by launching individual projects. Power structures between rich and poor countries, as well as elites in individual countries, are also the reason why we cannot eradicate poverty. Therefore, good governance is important, to do something about international structures and to ensure that natural resources benefit the population. Let me also underline the gradual turnaround of assistance to countries on the way out of a violent conflict and to countries facing specific climate challenges. I can mention Afghanistan and Sudan, and there are other examples, like Palestine. Finally, in the years I have worked on development policy and assistance, there are in particular three images that have been caught on the Internet: One is a visit to a United Nations-supported clinic in Maputo, supported by Norwegian money. They prevented HIV/AIDS, but they also had patients. The nurse looked us in the eyes and said, "The worst thing is to tell them that they're going to die." The second is when I gave talks on Norwegian equality policy for women politicians from six Andes countries in Lima, Peru. It was dead quiet in the hall. The girls put tears in their eyes, and they said afterwards, "We didn't know that was possible." And all ordered "Women Can" courses. This is also development policy — ensuring that girls gain power, that girls can become capable politicians and change the social structure. The third image I remember is our visit to Namibia, where with Norwegian help they have developed a well-functioning fisheries management regime. It hasn't cost us so much, either, but it was our knowledge, our competence, that ensured that the Namibian authorities could also develop a fisheries management regime. These are the areas in which we've engaged ourselves with our comparative advantage, and we're going to continue with that.

The sub-Saharan region of Africa is the most protectionist region in the world. 70 pst. of all trade barriers are introduced by poor countries – targeting poor people in other poor countries. Calculations show that if this kind of protectionism were to be abolished in southern Africa, it would immediately strengthen these countries' economies by well over $1 billion. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e2631	The Government’s development message is a comprehensive document covering a broad and important theme. I would also like to put myself in the order of those who give the case rapporteur, Højbräten, the honor of having carried out a very thorough processing of the notification. Over many years, Norway has been one of the world’s most aided countries, measured in dollars per year. the population. The overall objectives have been to contribute to poverty reduction, economic and social development and the promotion of human rights. There has been a significant degree of political agreement on the broad lines of development policy — albeit with some exceptions. St.meld. No 13 builds on and continues the main lines of Norwegian aid and development policy, while the Government in the Communication shows that the whole policy area and the debate around it are changing. In the context of the development and aid debate, both in the political environment and in the professional environment, the debate on how to allocate an ever-increasing aid budget has remained for too long. Reality has shown that such an approach is not enough. Over several years, it has become increasingly clear that aid has helped only to a limited extent to lift people out of poverty. Many of the main obstacles to development are beyond the scope of assistance. This has also been addressed by the Minister of Development and the Government in this Communication. The Government's willingness to think as a whole and a greater degree of coherent development policy are entirely positive. Nevertheless, the recognition that aid alone cannot create development has been a long way inside the Norwegian aid environment. Globalisation and international free trade are much stronger development engines than assistance, but this has had a limited impact on aid policy. Trust in assistance has remained high and the level of ambition has remained high. The Norwegian debate on assistance has focused on the size of the appropriations for official assistance, ODA assistance, typically in proportion to gross domestic product or national income – as a feature of a good poverty reduction policy. The government's violent focus on meeting its own one percentage point shows that old performances are still hard on. Instead of saying that it has worked positively, one might just as well argue that this notion has weakened the effect of the assistance. When the target is increased budgets, the need to evaluate results and improve efforts becomes less. The Minister for Development has himself stated that since the budgets are growing, there is no need to deprioritise anything. Such an attitude is not only unfortunate for the very part of development policy, since all policy at the end is about priorities, and because some things are actually more important than others, it is also unfortunate in the context of the legitimacy of development policy in the Norwegian people, and their willingness to pay for it. Nor have Norwegian authorities prioritised knowledge of how the aid works. After more than 40 years of Norwegian aid efforts, results reports were submitted only in 2007 and 2008 by NORAD. Unique responses to the proper functioning of the assistance are not provided in the reports. Right-wing wants a development policy that is measured in results rather than in percentages of GDP. This is precisely why it is so vital that Norway manages to pursue a coherent and coherent development policy. Several instruments need to be used, and several policy areas need to be considered in a coherent way if Norway is to contribute positively to development and poverty reduction. At the same time, the assistance we continue to provide must be more concentrated and targeted and subject to strict performance requirements. St.meld. No 13 is a positive signal of better changes in the first of these moments, but unfortunately fails in the second. In view of these overall lines, there are some aspects of the message itself that deserve closer discussion. One is the way in which the notification is incorporated into the policy pursued by the Minister for Development and the Environment in his years in the Council of State — or the chairs, to be accurate. Already in the first sentence of the Single European Act, the world’s biggest challenges are defined precisely as those Norway, according to the Government, have special prerequisites for solving. These councils' own work and interests are sometimes duplicated. The Storing Communication provides a broad description of the framework conditions for development, draws the long lines and the broad visions. At the same time, the descriptions are not followed up with concrete proposals for new measures or instruments in other policy areas to support development policy objectives. The government explicitly writes in the message: ‘Where necessary, concrete follow-up tools will be developed later’. This is a line both the Stortinget and the trade unions are constantly meeting with the Minister of Development. He prefers to operate on the overall level. Ministerial portfolios are mixed, land allocations and earmarked thematic budget lines are shifted to major regional aid budget appropriations. The transparency, participation and control of the size is reduced accordingly. To put it at the forefront, the Storting must take the necessary steps, so that a coherent and coherent development policy and an increased margin of action for the Government do not become a policy in which large parts of the budget are brought together and placed at the free disposal of the Council of State. Many seem to see a drift in that direction in the policy of this government and in this Council of State, not least in the area of aid policy. For this reason, the opposition stands together on comments that underline the need to further include the professional expertise built up over several decades – not least to avoid a politicising overarching which, in the worst case, may help to weaken the professional judgements and criteria that should be used as a basis for development and assistance policy. Short-term and politically motivated priorities must not undermine long-term assistance or assistance in general. This is important, because although development policy is more than aid, development policy is also aid – this year with a budget of €26.2 billion, a huge sum that obliges those who manage it. To this end, aid must be measured according to results, not according to the level of funding. Assistance should be concentrated, both thematically and geographically, not least in order to verify its effectiveness. Much more concrete performance targets need to be established and both monitoring and evaluation must be prioritised. The choice of the aid channel must be guided by the need to deliver results and efficiency. In another Storingsmessage Stortinget has for the moment to be treated, St.meld. No 15 for 2008-2009 on the broad lines of foreign policy, the Government writes on engagement policy, including assistance: «A common feature of the exposure policy is that it is demanding to measure and document results.» And on the other hand, ‘In order to remedy this, the Government therefore places considerable emphasis on performance orientation in the various policy areas. The challenges of documentation of results must never become any sleeping cushion that legitimizes a lack of focus on quality and efficiency.’ This last sentence right here will give its full support to. Unfortunately, it is more difficult to see that the Government, through its policy, is proposing to succeed in this. These weaknesses have been consistently highlighted by the right in relation to performance targets, monitoring and evaluation. So does the Riksrevision. In addition to the fact that such shortcomings have a sub-optimal effect on the recipient side, there is also a significant need to respond to voters’ requests for information and transparency on how to use the assistance money. In order for assistance to be effective, it is therefore necessary to develop more precise performance targets and to strengthen control of the achievement of the objectives. Some closing considerations, particularly in the signs of convergence: parallel to a significant assistance effort, Norway has maintained high customs barriers and defended a trade policy that creates effective barriers to developing countries’ export opportunities. This is a striking contradiction in Norwegian policy. The OECD has estimated that agricultural aid in developed countries represents many times all official aid. Norway, like so many other countries, gives with one hand and takes with the other. The experience of world history in the last hundred years shows that open economy, competition and market access contribute to the creation of growth and development. Exports and trade are so powerful drivers of development that Norway should no longer pursue a trade policy at the expense of the opportunities of poor countries. At the same time, development policy must help to strengthen the trade competence and capacity of poor countries. Given a better provision of development policy, the Right believes that rich countries such as Norway need to be prepared to increase efforts to combat poverty and contribute to more sustainable development. But increasing efforts does not mean increasing assistance. Increased efforts for development may be made in other ways and in other policy areas and budget chapters than by an increase in official development assistance.

Assistance is the little money that is going to trigger the big ones. The space for development is about power. Their demands led to smaller countries, more deregulations and privatisations, and less opportunities for land by sea to solve problems and be responsible to their own population for the solutions adopted. For the time being, they have won that battle, but the ministry has no financial means, despite the fact that aid should also go to women. The notification discusses capital flows, including illegal capital flows. This debate is highly topical but not new. The flow of money from colonial times up to our days has gone from south to north in the form of interest and repayments on loans, in the form that commodities are sold cheaply to the north, and in the form that transnational companies own resources in the south and serve themselves richly on it. The new thing is that the debate on illegal capital flows and tax havens has become clear on the international arena. I would like to commend the Minister of Finance and the Minister of Development who has the front line on the fight against tax havens. According to Eva Joly, the Norwegian government was the first to do so. Every year, the poorest countries lose billions because of tax evasion and tax competition. It is money that could have been spent on school, health and industrial development. So who's enriching? Both the political and the economic elite and the transnational companies do. This is much of the basis for corruption. Corruption had not gone on without places to hide the money. These include: therefore an offensive fight against tax havens is so important. In the long term, this work is being carried out in conjunction with efforts to increase the power of land navigation over its natural resources, to provide administrative regimes that made Norway rich, and to provide a political space for the adoption of laws and protection of weak industries and areas to be built up, as Norway and other countries have had the opportunity to do, some of the most important steps to lift people out of poverty and to decent lives. It is no longer possible to close the eyes of the greatest crisis in the world. Man-made climate change threatens tomorrow. The poorest are threatened today. No other matter is more urgent to do something about. If we don't solve the climate crisis, neither do the world the Millennium Goals. Not only must the world’s richest countries take the greatest responsibility for resolving the climate crisis by reducing CO2 emissions, but the world’s richest countries must also take the primary responsibility for enabling developing countries to adapt to the effects of climate change. Because when the world's poorest are exposed to drought, violent storms, floods and environmental pressures, this means that they have less resources left to create a better life for themselves and their children. When global warming changes the weather conditions on the Horn of Africa, it means that crops fail and people starve, or that women and young girls need to spend several hours collecting water. We in the rich countries must take the greatest responsibility for reducing climate emissions, developing renewable energy, find predictable mechanisms to finance technologies in countries that have the same right to a decent standard of living as we have, but without going the road to CO2 emissions – and not least to find a mechanism to ensure poor adaptability. The world needs to find solutions to the Copenhagen climate meeting. There is a need to find new ways of financing. An example is the anticipated proposal by the Minister of Finance to auction away part of the global climate allowances for this. The fight against climate change is both local and global. Before the death of Brazilian environmental defender Chico Mendes, he talked about which bands tied the local struggle to preserve the rainforest to a global struggle for social justice: «At first, I thought I was fighting to save rubber trees. Then I thought I was fighting to save the rain forest in the Amazon. Now I understand that I am fighting for humanity." Norway is on the verge of saving the rainforest. Forest activity is important for climate, biodiversity and the livelihood of indigenous people. It reduces poverty, whether in Brazil, Papua New Guinea or Africa. The cancellation of the debt following the shipping campaign was historic. If there's something I want to remember with joy, it's just this. There was a hurries up through the international debt cancellation movement when Development Minister Erik Solheim announced this debt cancellation, because then illegitimate debt came on the international agenda. This is something we in SV have fought for too long, so I am proud of what was done. It is not fair for today’s population in indebted countries to continue to pay the price of corrupt, reckless or politically motivated lending that has continued. The Committee commends the Minister for Development on the initiative of Norway vis-à-vis the World Bank and other countries. This work needs to be intensified. Debtslette provides more resources for important tasks, and it strengthens democracy by making it less bound by the World Bank, the IMF and other donors.

The requirement of specific Norwegian competence must not prevent Norway from contributing bilaterally also in areas that are recognised as important internationally. The education field is one of these, an area in which the Stortinget believes Norway should have increased efforts. The representative Marit Nybakk pointed to a lucky project in Namibia. It was about fishing. That's where we have specific competence. We may also have specific competence in aquaculture development. We were part of that in Vietnam. That's where we were asked to be more involved in Namibia. Agriculture is an area where, in a global context, we may not have specific competence, but I think it is important that we also focus on it. I'm glad we're in St.meld. No 13 – at least as the Centre Party interprets it – has changed its view on this with food production. When we measured the distribution between different sectors in 1970, it was 30 pst. of Norwegian efforts aimed at food production. Today it's at 3 pst. We're in a global food crisis. I don't think we'll be able to deal with many of the problems we're facing if we don't enable the people of developing countries to feed themselves to a greater extent than today. We have to be able to take that challenge. But it will also have consequences for the thinking here on the mountain. I think we need to be involved in developing research to look at how we're going to exploit the limited areas that we have in the world today, in order to achieve sustainable development with even better conclusions. I think the message is interesting, as I have said. It's exciting, and I imagine there will be a lot of important debates in this area in this room in time to come.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e2742	Initially, I would also like to thank Mr Højbräten for constructive processes in the Committee, and I would like to commend the Minister for the Environment and Development for presenting an interesting and thought-provoking communication. It provides a broad description of the framework conditions for development work and points out whether or not new policies, so at least policy areas that should be more closely integrated into Norwegian development policies. We should all get our eyes up so that development policy is something much more than assistance. This can make a valuable contribution to the debate on development policy in Norway. The debate may continue to look beyond the 1 pst target, and some are still hanging around in a narrow debate on whether or not assistance is working at all. However, the Communication is somewhat lingering in proposing concrete measures to follow up the policy contained in the Communication. Instead, it refers to the on-going processes and the forums for following up the themes in question. This applies, inter alia, to: important measures in agriculture and trade. Furthermore, the communication is likely to fail somewhat in terms of promoting a more holistic development policy. This requires action in other policy areas to ensure coherence. Policies implemented in other areas need to be adapted so that they do not weaken or undermine development policy. I see in the communication progress towards creating a greater political space for the Government in the field of aid policy. It is worth mentioning here that it has already replaced the country allocation in the aid budget with regional appropriations. This gives the Government greater freedom to redispatch aid between countries in a region, for example: in Africa. Furthermore, the Government provides more flexibility to provide humanitarian and development assistance in post-conflict areas, where it has been involved in peace processes. This isn't necessarily negative. However, I miss an emphasis on the technical assistance – and the Norwegian competence built up in this area over several years with assistance. I believe that we should avoid further politicisation of assistance. It may help to undermine the sound peer-reviews that should be used as a basis for all assistance. This may weaken the long-term assistance and the quality of assistance in general. Furthermore, we should avoid further politicisation and a rapidly changing Norwegian agenda related to development policy undermining the principle of local ownership for own development. Developing countries should themselves govern and own their development, not be governed by well-meaning donors in Norway and in other donor countries. In order for this to make sense, I believe that assistance should contribute to strengthening representative, inclusive and democratic processes in the recipient countries. I therefore agree that efforts in Norwegian aid should be stepped up in order to lay the foundations for more robust party systems and more vibrant democratic processes in the recipient countries. In order to ensure a broad political response to relatively high aid allocations in the future, it is important to be able to demonstrate efficiency, credibility and concrete results. The Left therefore believes that the appropriations for quality assurance and evaluation should be increased – based on a low proportion of the aid budget today. We spend 26 billion dollars in aid this year. It has had a formidable growth over many years, and this requires that we have proper control of the use of these means. More specifically, I believe that increased funds should be used for independent quality assurance and evaluation. The objective should be to identify errors and deficiencies related to ongoing and final assistance projects, and this information should be more systematically included in the decision-making basis for future assistance. Furthermore, the appropriations should be used to further develop new methods of evaluation and quality assurance, both at national level and in cooperation with other donor countries. In addition, beneficiaries of the assistance should be more closely involved in the evaluations. They're the ones who have to live with the results of the projects, and they know best if they're working or not. I appreciate the ability and will for innovation in development policy. Nevertheless, I believe that the UN's Millennium Goals should remain a guide to Norwegian aid. Among these objectives, I believe that Norway’s contribution to promoting education and health should be strengthened. This is fundamental in a country. More specifically, there should be a strong and clear focus on women, children, young people and vulnerable groups, including sexual and reproductive health and HIV prevention. In addition, the climate crisis must not be downgraded in relation to developing countries. Norway has a significant responsibility here. Finally, I would like to take the opportunity to stress that the rainforest effort should not be at the expense of following up on the Government's strategy for environmental development cooperation. It is important to follow up both parts, and therefore one should not become a balancing item for the other.

Those decisions affect human rights, employee rights, the environment and climate. SPU is now the joint owner of so many companies that they collectively affect the economic development of the world. It gives Norway the power to create a business development with a more socially responsible perspective, and with power comes responsibility and great opportunities. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e2899	An increasing number of questions are now asking about the significance of the assistance for the various countries that have over the years received gifts of money from so-called rich or industrialised countries. It is important to stress that developing countries themselves have the primary responsibility for their own development, and that in many countries policies are in line with economic growth and poverty reduction targets. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e2925	To begin with, I would like to make a general comment: I think it is commendable that both the notification and the vote describe the complexity of the possibilities for creating developments and the contributions that Norway can make in this context, and the dependence on international framework conditions, geopolitical changes and the need for coherence in policy-making and conflicting interests at national and international level. It points out that the dominant image globally is that countries with democratic systems have the best results both in terms of economic and social development, not to mention when it comes to safeguarding fundamental freedoms and rights. Democracy is the very basis for national ownership of a country’s development. It's a goal in itself. In development aid, we have always heard about the need to strengthen civil society and support non-governmental organisations, but rarely has there been any emphasis on elected political bodies and parliaments and the need to involve them more strongly in the implementation of aid policy. It provides opportunities for wider ownership of development, better public participation and is the source of debate and transparency – yes, in short, elected bodies are a crucial part of democracy and good governance. So here I mean we're on safe ground when we push. But it just goes beyond representation. Today, the design and implementation of assistance in the dialogue between government and administrative apparatus on the recipient and donor side is taking place. Responsibility for national implementation goes from government to government, without involving national parliaments in the recipient country. I am therefore pleased that the Committee believes that it must be a premise for the assistance that Parliament in the country of destination is involved in the political debate on the country's development objectives, the prioritisation of means of assistance and the control of the Governments' use of funds. When an increase in the amount of budgetary support is sought, the need for popular participation is further reinforced. Norway must therefore be an exponent of stronger parliamentary involvement, but this requires that we be willing to strengthen Parliament's capacity in the recipient countries – both in terms of knowledge and resources. In a unanimous opinion of the Council of Europe now in spring on the mobilisation of parliaments both in Africa and in Europe for Africa's development, it was recommended to earmark assistance to strengthen parliamentary participation in the planning and control of aid. However, representative parliaments require democracies with active political parties. Therefore, there is a need to support direct cooperation between Norwegian political parties and their sister parties in developing countries. Here I would like to ask the Progress Party: why should other non-governmental organisations be responsible for the transfer of knowledge between political parties? In this I know a bit of the scepticism that I feel has been in involving political parties in the development of good governance in our developing countries. Such cooperation must be based on the individual experience and competence of the parties and assessed individually. I am pleased that the Committee believes that there should be no requirement for cross-political participation or for more parties to the projects. Political parties are also crucial elements of democracy and good governance. Employment, decent employment and the protection of workers' rights are key values in the development of an economically and socially equitable society. This perspective must have an important place in aid policy. I want to commend the Government's new strategy to strengthen employee rights globally, where the ILO is the main body. This concerns, among other things: on the fight against child labour, on equality and women's rights in the labour market, on strengthening the social partners and cooperation between them. Development policy must focus on decent working life. But not just here, you have to do it in trade policy and foreign policy in general. We have the opportunity to repeat this message in the discussion of the vote on Norwegian foreign policy until Friday. There is considerable social and political capital in the Norwegian/Nordic model in the face of globalisation and aid for development.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e2948	As one of the richest countries in the world, it is only fair and reasonable that Norway also begs for other countries to develop. We have a long and in my view a good tradition of providing assistance to developing countries while being an important supporter of the UN and other international organisations. The Committee's position shows that Norway will continue to be an important aid operator in the future. I am pleased with the broad support given to the Government by the Committee. Most people who have spoken today have suggested that we need a wide range of nutrition and a broad range of tools to achieve the goal of a fairer value. Development policy is much more than assistance. I think it is good for the debate that there is so much agreement about this in this Chamber. It has long been a political goal of 1 pst. our national income shall be granted assistance. This goal has now reached us for the first time, and it is very good. The goal has been decisive to get the rewards in this area. How much money Norway and other countries are willing to spend on assistance is crucial. Nevertheless, we must not be so preoccupied with the quantity that we forget about the quality. Therefore, Norwegian aid needs to be measured, not just according to the amount of money that Stortinget allows each year, but on the impact of these funds. Good control systems and evaluation of money use are therefore very important. It is also important to have a wide and lively debate on what actions work and what actions do not work. It is good for a Joint Committee to clarify the need for independent evaluation and research. It's important that the government follows up at this point. It is also important that there are no different policies in any other part. Getting a more coherent policy is therefore important. An annual report on how different policy areas affect developments in poor countries is therefore a good initiative by the Government. Such reporting will increase the focus and debate on what actions contribute to development and what may hinder development. In line with the idea of making the most efficient and targeted use of resources, it is right to focus more on those tasks where Norway has specific skills and knowledge, while being an active contributor to the multinational organisations where Norway does not have specific competences that will provide added value. In this way, we not only encourage these organisations to increase their impact on the economy, but also encourage fewer actors and improve international coordination in the various countries. Norway has specific competence in several areas. The transfer of competence, knowledge and technology may in some cases be as important as the transfer of heads and ears. Several members of the Committee have had the positive experience of fisheries cooperation presented by the Committee in Namibia. For relatively cheap money, Norway has contributed to the development of a national fisheries policy and a national fisheries policy in Namibia. This has resulted in important revenues for the country, while at the same time contributing to more sustainable exploitation of fisheries resources outside Namibia. Another area in which Norway has special competence is within energy. We have both a knowledge of how to produce and not least a knowledge of how to manage energy resources in a way that both takes into account the environment and leads to openness in the use of income and a reasonable distribution of income among residents. Here we can transfer knowledge that can far exceed the value in heads and ears. The objective of development policy is to contribute to the success of several countries. Therefore, aid to industrial development and own income is crucial. Management of natural resources is therefore important, and Norway has specific competences. At the same time, I agree with the Council when he says that we are not here in the country to define what kind of knowledge we should contribute to, but the demand from the recipient countries. It's an important principle. When I'm a big fan of Norway's being a great aid actor, it's not just because I think we have a moral responsibility to be. I also believe that in this country we have clear benefits. The fight against nudity and poverty is also the fight against war and terrorism, against large refugee traumas and against devastation and climate change. Less poverty will provide a more secure value that we in Norway too will benefit from.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e2967	Nearly one billion people are starving. It's about every six people living on Earth. The hunger causes malnourished mothers to give birth to malnourished children. Food is a fundamental human right that millions of people are denied every day despite the fact that the world is currently producing enough food. Ability for self-help and independence presupposes that people can eat saturated. Climate change reinforces unfairness. The Earth is getting warmer, and the projections for the future are serious. All over the world, floods, extreme weather and disease will increase. Agriculture lives with and from the climate. Those exposed to drought will be hit even harder in the future. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change expects African agriculture to deteriorate because temperature increases make large agricultural areas unfit for food production. I am pleased that Minister of Agriculture Lars Peder Breck says that he will work to ensure that food and food production becomes an important topic at the international climate conference in Copenhagen in December. Something else would be a betrayal of both climate and world hunger. Agricultural aid needs to be re-increased. We need agricultural assistance that can provide robust agriculture with a property right that is spread over the many hands, and independence to operate ecologically soundly. Still the world's farmers produce enough food for everyone, but something's wrong with the way we distribute the food. Today it is estimated that almost half of those who starve are food producers. Continuing what's now, it's also uncertain whether the world is able to produce enough food for everyone, sail if we distribute it fairly. The race if the food earth is running. For many years, most people thought that commodity prices on food would just sink. Last year food prices rose sharply. Prices have now fallen and stabilised, but the analysts believe that prices will rise in the coming years. It is due to climate change and increased use of food for biofuels. Development has made arable land an investment object. Investors from Saudi Arabia rent land in Ethiopia to produce rice, oats and wheat. All production is exported to Saudi Arabia and investors are tax-free. At the same time, the World Food Programme provides food aid to Ethiopia. China has purchased large areas of Congo to produce biofuels of palm oil. South Korea and the United Arab Emirates have made huge purchases in Sudan, while many die of hunger in the country they are going to fetch their food from. By 2050 it is assumed that there will be 2.4 billion more people on earth than today. In India and China, more meat is eaten, and the demand for food is increasing fastest here. Both countries have limited opportunities to expand their productive area. However, areas are available in sub-Saharan Africa and South and Central America. Arable land must be kept in all countries. It is contrary to any joint thinking and common sense to lay down arable land in Norway or elsewhere when the need for soil for food production will increase dramatically. If we are to reduce hunger, the world's food policy needs to change, and that involves a whole new policy in the WTO. The starting point must be that all countries have both the right and the duty to feed their own people. In order to ensure this, it is necessary to establish trade rules distinguishing between food production for export and food production intended to cover national consumption. We have to stop the evolution we see, where full food is exported to animal feed or fuel in the western world from countries where the population needs food. It is as obvious that export subsidies must be prohibited because Western subsidised goods often compete with local production. Foreign investors and multinational companies cannot continue a cynical trade in food as if they were soon to be one billion hungry. The climate and fair distribution of food and food security must also become a critical part of the WTO agenda.
