ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e3153	Schools have been one of the most debated topics in the media and in political circles for over a year. Peace, order, discipline and a good learning environment have been important keywords. It is also the theme of this matter. Regrets and unrest are, unfortunately, a major problem in school. Actionable school staff have been requested and measures taken to repatriate the teacher as requested by the official. Of course, it is the responsibility of the school and the individual teacher to enforce the regulations and ensure good working conditions in the classroom. Of course, each student has obligations to comply with the rules in force at school. It is then also a joint committee which considers it absolutely necessary to follow up infringements of the Rules of Procedure in the form of penalties. It is very positive that schools address the problem and, through different instruments, try to find solutions to different situations. Teachers must have the opportunity to do their job, to teach. Students are also entitled to a good, well-functioning learning environment. Some schools have established rules of procedure in which students' rights and duties are described and made mutually binding for students, the school and its directors. This is also the background to this case, and the case under which the Directorate of Education's decision is based, where the Directorate of Education is, in other words, tightening the rules of the Rules of Procedure. Based on the school regulations, a practice has been introduced in which students who do not meet exactly to the hour are referred to the library for the remainder of the hour. It must be added that alternative teaching arrangements are in place at the library with environmental work, where students who have been expelled from education can work for the rest of the hour. The County Council considered that this scheme is pedagogically sound, subject to systematic follow-up of pupils who are in breach of the school regulations. The Committee agrees. There is unnecessary bureaucracy and poor use of school resources if only the principal has the authority to expel students from individual hours. Such powers should be delegated to the teachers. Such practice lies within the scope of action which individual teachers must have in order to exercise authority and class management. The effect of an immediate reaction to a rule violation is itself a point. On 8 May 2009, the Committee received letters from the Council of State for Solhjell supporting the Committee's position: I quote from the letter written in book goals: ‘I believe that the decision to reject individual hours should be able to be delegated from the principal to the teachers. The Ministry will therefore, at the hearing, submit a proposal to amend the rules of the Training Act and the Private School Act which provide for the delegation of a disfellowshipping authority for individual hours from the principal to the professionals.’;» I am very pleased that the Council of State has followed up this matter very quickly. The legislative proposal has already been drawn up and sent to the hearing on Friday 5 June. The proposal states, inter alia, that: the following: ‘The rector may, however, decide that teachers shall have the means to decide whether to reject education for a period of up to one day.’ That's good. And that's also this committee wants. Nevertheless, the minority has made a proposal that is identical to the one already initiated by the Government and actually implemented. Since the mission has already come a long way in the process, I allow myself to say that the minority proposal seems somewhat redundant. It's actually going to be opening wide-open doors.
ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e3185	I would like to thank the President-in-Office for a good review until the last sentence to open wide doors. The rapporteur could have saved himself, but otherwise it was quite good. It has been common practice in Norwegian schools that teachers have the power to expel pupils from single hours if they break the rules of procedure. The system is important both to ensure the ability of the teacher to sanction non-regulatory students and to ensure the teacher’s authority in the classroom. There has never been a political dispute about the teacher's right to expel students, and the debate was probably somewhat surprising when the Education Directorate interpreted the current legislation so that expulsion from individual hours was not permitted. Since this practice has been followed for a very long time, and in the obvious belief that one has the right to do so, one could argue that the scheme is established in customary law, or that the intervention is so insignificant that it does not require a separate authorisation for the implementation of individual hours of expulsion. However, the Directorate-General for Education considers that separate grounds are necessary. There is an interpretation that the department obviously agrees with, and there is a notice of legislative change, as the rapporteur explained. It should therefore be expected that today's Stortinget proposal would actually receive unanimous agreement, so that the Council of State does not undertake a work which has already been voted down by the Assembly. However, the Progress Party believes that one does not need to wait for a legislative proposal from the Government because the representatives of the Storting can choose a more efficient and less bureaucratic solution. It is the Stortinget that is the legislative authority and in any case has to deal with this matter. For this reason, the Progress Party has promoted a legislative proposal that will provide a basis for individual hours of expulsion. That proposal is under consideration in Odelstinget later today. ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e3480	The Progress Party addresses a very important issue in this matter. There is no doubt that being bullyed can set lasting tracks. The background was that we saw an increasing incidence of bullying in Norwegian schools, and the strong media focus with many strong stories helped to make this known. Both King Harald and then Prime Minister Bondevik held New Year's speech on bullying, and schools were offered the anti- bullying programs that had documented effect. There were many at the time who disagreed about launching a zero vision, because they thought it was unrealistic. But the strong focus and ambitious goals that were set at that time produced results. While the bullying increased by an average of 10 pst. in the year 1995–2001, bullying was reduced by 30 pst in 2004. Unfortunately, we see that the pressure and focus have disappeared in recent years, and that the number of bullying has increased again. The school offer is currently being financed as a framework funding for municipalities and counties. Within the framework set out in the annual budgets, municipalities shall provide the basic and important welfare services in schools and care for citizens. This scheme is based on a fundamental idea that it is the individual municipality itself that is best able to assess the organisation and quality of the offer to be made. The result of this funding mechanism is that the provision of school care and care is very diverse in the more than 430 different municipalities and 19 counties doing things their way. They give priority to what they think is most important. In addition to the basic offers, we see that many municipalities prioritise a number of other things – investments in shares, cultural houses and large, fine town halls. The Party considers that education is such a fundamental right that everyone should be entitled to a qualitatively good offer regardless of their place of residence. In order to achieve such a guarantee, it is essential that the financing of the offer is modified. Only by introducing a State, directly differentiated, unit price to go to the individual school via each student will a predictable school operating framework be created, based on national priorities and quality requirements. This does not mean – as the government parties believe – a massive privatisation of schools in the country, but a safe and secure financing, regardless of who owns them. The municipalities and the counties will largely remain school owners, but they will not cut school budgets when the community economy goes bad. A number of municipalities and counties have already introduced a form of price financing for schools, and experience is largely good. The point of progress makes it clear that there is a differentiated unit price that is the solution, so that a student who has, for example: dyslexia, has a higher price because more effort is needed in learning. The same applies to foreign-language students and a number of others who, for different reasons, need increased school follow-up. In this way, these students become sought after by the schools, and the schools are given automatic resources to provide the student with what they are entitled to. In today's system, schools don't get one more ear for students who need this follow-up, and the result is that students get a worse offer than they need. We believe that the freedom of choice of students is a fundamental principle, and that some competition between schools is healthy. The Committee also noted that the Riksrevision promoted criticism of the Ministry of Knowledge in Document 3:14 for 2007-2008. The National Auditorate wrote, among other things:\t\t You can change the ‘The audit of the Court of Auditors shows that, overall, significant unsolved tasks related to adult participation in initial training remain.’ The Committee is aware that the Ministry has subsequently initiated certain measures to follow up the referrals of the Riksrevision. The Committee agrees on all the essentials of facts and the importance of future focus on actions that can strengthen the basic skills of the adult population. However, the majority of the Committee, consisting of government parties, refers to Ot.prp. 87 for 2008-2009 and to a forthcoming EEA Communication, which will assess actions in an overall context. The opposition is more impatient and, on its side, stands together for five concrete proposals. The Progress Party and the Right agree on another proposal. I assume that the representatives of the majority and the proposers themselves will supplement the content as necessary. I shall then move on to comment on the one proposal on which the Progress Party stands alone. The above-mentioned criticism from the National Audit Office also points out that one of the reasons for the lack of follow-up and control of the rights of adults to initial training is due to the funding system. Today, municipalities and counties are required to finance initial training for adults, and the Progress Party believes this in itself helps each municipality and state to avoid costs by preventing the publication of adult law. About this, the Riksrevision writes the following: ‘The study shows that there is still a debate on the financing of the tasks related to organisation, information and motivation, which are considered as key prerequisites for implementing primary school law. The study also shows that several municipalities justify a lack of primary school provision that the State has not provided additional resources.’ Of these, the Progress Party points out that the main and most effective measure to ensure a better real opportunity to exercise the right to initial training for adults is State funding of the offer. The proposal of the Progress Party is thus addressed.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e3965	Government parties need to briefly justify their views on these proposals, in addition to what appears in the suspension. First, however, I would like to thank the rapporteur for the good and effective work he has done on this proposal. In order to meet the labour market challenges arising from the financial crisis, the Government is pursuing an active labour market policy. In particular, it is important to avoid increased leave leading to long leave periods for the individual. One of the main objectives of the Nav reform is to increase work and activity and reduce social benefits. The labour and welfare management must also ensure the functioning of the income protection system. The unemployed are entitled to a daily allowance, and they will receive it quickly. So some comments on the proposals: the first proposal involves halving the proportion of businesses to increase the skills of employees. At present, intra-corporate training is provided in the form of training grants to the enterprise for the costs of training and grants for wage costs to the participant. The rules for intra-company training clearly distinguish between support for general and specific training. The proposal does not identify which of these types of training is requested by the proposers. In addition, the EU has set maximum limits for the support that can be provided through intra-corporate training. Support for specific training is regulated by percentage according to the size of the enterprise. In the case of making the permitting arrangements more flexible, so that 20 pst. Permitting is made possible in practice, we are sceptical about this. From 1 July the Government will provide that the requirement for working time reductions shall be 40 pst. in the case of permitted persons. If the requirement to reduce working time is reduced to 20 pst., the total number of workers could be compensated for by a very high level of compensation in the event of reduced work. He/she will then be able to receive full pay four days a week and in addition more than 60 pst. daily allowances for the last day. There will then be a risk that some employers and employees will comply with such partial leave. Then a proposal for the TaxFUNN and the possibilities to include projects in a company or between companies, providing increased competence, is promoted. Due to the design of State aid legislation, it will not now be possible to include these types of projects in the tax FUNN, so that it may need to be further assessed. There are also today several schemes that are likely to respond to the same needs of businesses. Furthermore, it is proposed that competent staff should be taught in secondary schools. There is currently nothing to prevent the employee from taking other work during the period of leave, for example: secondary education, during the period of leave. It follows from the conditions for entitlement to the daily allowance that you are willing to take another job. It is also proposed to increase the allocation of basic labour skills. To that end, in recent years, the Government has provided for a strong stepping-up of the Programme of Basic Skills in the labour market in order to give job seekers and workers greater opportunity to acquire the skills deemed necessary in modern working life. This year, as a whole of the crisis packages and the draft revised national budget, 80 million will be allocated. available for basic labour skills, which is more than a doubling of the 2008 effort.

There is no doubt that SkattenFUNN is primarily intended for research and development, but it would not hurt to find out whether the focus on training and competence could be raised by providing opportunities for either single companies or enterprises in cooperation, when the projects are quality assured. So to reverse the tightening up of labour rent legislation between production companies. The introduction of such a restriction was probably motivated by the fear of social dumping. But social dumping should be stopped in completely different ways than reducing the flexibility of the labour market we have. In this respect, potentially many manufacturing companies may either have to permit or dismiss more employees than should be strictly necessary. So such a reversal should definitely be in place. As far as school and business are concerned, we know what challenges we have. It's obvious that it's a school owner's task, but what we want to focus on is if not the challenge is so big that it should simply develop a national strategy to get it speeded up. The last relates to additional appropriations for the BKA. We know it's about 137 million. kr to BKA, and that the allocation on the State budget is far from satisfactory in relation to the need out there. And with reference to the discussion in the previous case, it might be through the company that it gives a very good opportunity to reach that group. I'm addressing proposal No. 6 in the setting.

ParlaMint-NO_2009-06-08.ana.ud2315e4041	There are again important questions raised by the proposers here, and I would like to make it clear that several of the proposals are within the remit of other government councils, but I answer on behalf of the Government and have consulted me with both the Minister of Labour and the Minister of Inclusion and the Minister of Economic Affairs and Trade. The economic downturn that we're now part of is perhaps the strongest downturn we've seen on this side of the Second World War. For this reason, the Government has also taken proportionate measures, in the first round, by making the financial market work as fully as possible, by reducing interest rates, in the second round, through extensive measures to ensure that the jobs it has are kept as much as possible, whether in the private sector or in the public sector, and, of course, in what one can call a third round through an extensive commitment to competence. Competence efforts have been made to both reduce the number of unemployed people, provide an alternative for those who are unemployed, and, in the long term, increase access to the labour market and increase the level of competence in society. Thus, there is a background for some of the measures of different types that have been implemented.
